# Far-right politics



[Alt-right](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Alt-right) members preparing at the 2017 [Unite the Right rally](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Unite_the_Right_rally) in [Charlottesville, Virginia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Charlottesville,_Virginia). Participants are carrying [Gadsden flags](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Gadsden_flag), a [Nazi flag](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Flag_of_Nazi_Germany), and [Confederate battle flags](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Modern_display_of_the_Confederate_battle_flag).

**Far-right politics**[^cite_note-1] are politics further to the right on the [political spectrum](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Left-right_political_spectrum) than the standard [political right](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Political_right). Far-right politics encompass a range of ideologies that are marked by [ultraconservatism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ultraconservatism), [ultranationalism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ultranationalism),[^cite_note-7] [authoritarianism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Authoritarianism), and [radical anti-communism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Radical_anti-communism).[^cite_note-8]

This part of the political spectrum is on the far end of the right, distinguished from more mainstream [right-wing](56522.md) ideologies by its emphasis on [postliberalist](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Postliberalism) and [exclusivist](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Exclusivism) views or opposition to [liberal democracy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Liberal_democracy). Far-right ideologies have historically included [reactionary](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Reactionary) [conservatism](6675.md), [fascism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fascism), and [Nazism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nazism), while contemporary manifestations also incorporate [neo-fascism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Neo-fascism), [neo-Nazism](54361.md), [supremacism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Supremacism), and various other movements characterized by [chauvinism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Chauvinism), [xenophobia](34141.md), and [theocratic](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Theocratic) or reactionary beliefs. [Far-right terrorism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Far-right_terrorism) consists of [extremist](428002.md), [militant](310790.md), or [insurgent](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Insurgent) groups that attempt to realise their ideals through [political violence](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Political_violence) rather than using democratic processes.

Key to the far-right worldview is the notion of societal purity, often invoking ideas of a homogeneous community based on cultural, ethnic, national, racial, or religious lines or a combination of them. This view generally promotes [organicism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Organicism), which perceives society as a unified, natural entity under threat from [diversity](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Diversity_\(politics\)) or modern [pluralism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pluralism_\(political_philosophy\)). Far-right movements frequently target perceived threats to their idealized community, whether racial, ethnic, religious, or cultural, leading to [anti-immigrant sentiments](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Anti-immigrant_sentiment), [welfare chauvinism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Welfare_chauvinism), and, in extreme cases, political violence or oppression.[^cite_note-9] According to political theorists, the far right appeals to those who believe in maintaining strict cultural and ethnic divisions and a return to traditional social hierarchies and values.[^cite_note-10]

In practice, far-right movements differ widely by region and historical context. In Western Europe, they have often focused on anti-immigration and [anti-globalism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Right-wing_antiglobalism), while in Eastern Europe, strong [anti-communist](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Anti-communism) rhetoric is more common. The United States has seen a unique evolution of far-right movements that emphasize nativism and radical opposition to an increasingly centralized federal government.

&#x20;Far-right politics have often led to [oppression](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Oppression), [political violence](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Political_violence), [forced assimilation](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Forced_assimilation), [ethnic cleansing](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ethnic_cleansing), and [genocide](12441.md) against groups of people based on their supposed inferiority or their perceived threat to the native [ethnic group](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ethnic_group), [nation](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nation), [state](23604120.md), [national religion](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Religious_nationalism), [dominant culture](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Dominant_culture), or [conservative](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Traditionalist_conservatism) social institutions.[^cite_note-11] Across these contexts, far-right politics has continued to influence discourse, occasionally achieving electoral success and prompting significant debate over its place in [democratic societies](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Democratic_societies).

According to scholars [Jean-Yves Camus](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jean-Yves_Camus) and [Nicolas Lebourg](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nicolas_Lebourg), the core of the far right's worldview is [organicism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Organicism), the idea that society functions as a complete, organized, and homogeneous living being. Adapted to the community they wish to constitute or reconstitute (whether based on ethnicity, nationality, religion or race), the concept leads them to reject every form of [universalism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Universalism) in favor of *[autophilia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Autophilia)* and *alterophobia*, or in other words the idealization of a "we" excluding a "they".[^cite_note-FOOTNOTECamusLebourg201722-12] The far right tends to absolutize differences between nations, races, individuals, or cultures since they disrupt their efforts towards the [utopian](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Utopia) dream of the "closed" and naturally organized society, perceived as the condition to ensure the rebirth of a community finally reconnected to its quasi-eternal nature and re-established on firm [metaphysical](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Metaphysical) foundations.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTECamusLebourg201721-13][^cite_note-FOOTNOTEBar-On2016xiii-14]

As they view their community in a state of decay facilitated by the ruling elites, far-right members portray themselves as a natural, sane and alternative elite, with the redemptive mission of saving society from its promised doom. They reject both their national political system and the global geopolitical order (including their institutions and values, e.g. [political liberalism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Political_liberalism) and [egalitarian](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Egalitarian) [humanism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Humanism)) which are presented as needing to be abandoned or purged of their impurities, so that the "redemptive community" can eventually leave the current phase of [liminal](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Liminality) crisis to usher in the new era.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTECamusLebourg201722-12][^cite_note-FOOTNOTEBar-On2016xiii-14] The community itself is idealized through great [archetypal](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Archetype) figures (the [Golden Age](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Golden_Age), the savior, [decadence](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Decadence) and global [conspiracy theories](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Conspiracy_theories)) as they glorify [non-rationalistic](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Irrationalism) and non-[materialistic](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Materialism) values such as the youth or the cult of the dead.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTECamusLebourg201722-12]

Political scientist [Cas Mudde](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cas_Mudde) argues that the far right can be viewed as a combination of four broadly defined concepts, namely [exclusivism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Exclusivism) (e.g. [racism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Racism), [xenophobia](34141.md), [ethnocentrism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ethnocentrism), [ethnopluralism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ethnopluralism), [chauvinism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Chauvinism), including [welfare chauvinism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Welfare_chauvinism)), [anti-democratic](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Anti-democratic) and non-individualist traits (e.g. [cult of personality](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cult_of_personality), [hierarchism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hierarchism), [monism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Monism), [populism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Populism), [anti-particracy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Anti-particracy), an [organicist](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Organicist) view of the state), a [traditionalist](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Traditionalist_conservatism) value system lamenting the disappearance of historic frames of reference (e.g. [law and order](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Law_and_order_\(politics\)), the family, the ethnic, linguistic and religious community and nation as well as the [natural environment](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Natural_environment)[^cite_note-15]) and a socioeconomic program associating [corporatism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Corporatism), state control of certain sectors, [agrarianism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Agrarianism), and a varying degree of belief in the free play of [socially Darwinistic](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Socially_Darwinistic) market forces. Mudde then proposes a subdivision of the far-right nebula into moderate and radical leanings, according to their degree of exclusionism and [essentialism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Essentialism).[^cite_note-16][^cite_note-FOOTNOTECamusLebourg201744–45-17]

### Definition and comparative analysis

The *Encyclopedia of Politics: The Left and the Right* states that far-right politics include "persons or groups who hold extreme nationalist, xenophobic, homophobic, racist, religious fundamentalist, or other reactionary views". While the term *far right* is typically applied to [fascists](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fascists) and [neo-Nazis](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Neo-Nazis), it has also been used to refer to those to the right of mainstream [right-wing politics](56522.md).[^cite_note-FOOTNOTECarlisle2005694-18]

According to political scientist Lubomír Kopeček, "\[t]he best working definition of the contemporary far right may be the four-element combination of nationalism, xenophobia, law and order, and welfare chauvinism proposed for the Western European environment by Cas Mudde."[^cite_note-Kopeček_2007-19] Relying on those concepts, far-right politics includes yet is not limited to aspects of authoritarianism, anti-communism,[^cite_note-Kopeček_2007-19] and [nativism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nativism_\(politics\)).[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEHilliardKeith199943-20] Claims that superior people should have greater rights than inferior people are often associated with the far right, as they have historically favored a social Darwinistic or [elitist](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Elitist) hierarchy based on the belief in the legitimacy of the rule of a supposedly superior minority over the inferior masses.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEWoshinsky2008154–155-21] Regarding the socio-cultural dimension of nationality, culture and migration, one far-right position is the view that certain ethnic, racial, or religious groups should stay separate, based on the belief that the interests of one's own group should be prioritized.[^cite_note-Anders1-22]

In Western Europe, far-right parties have been associated with [anti-immigrant](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nativism_\(politics\)) policies, as well as opposition to [globalism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Globalism) and [European integration](683704.md). They often make nationalist and [xenophobic](34141.md) appeals which make allusions to [ethnic nationalism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ethnic_nationalism) rather than [civic nationalism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Civic_nationalism) (or liberal nationalism). Some have at their core [illiberal policies](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Illiberal_democracy), such as removing [checks on executive authority](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Checks_and_Balances), and protections for minorities from majority ([multipluralism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pluralism_\(philosophy\))). In the 1990s, the "winning formula" was often to attract anti-immigrant [blue collar workers](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Blue-collar_worker) and [white collar workers](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/White-collar_worker) who wanted less state intervention in the economy, but in the 2000s, this switched to [welfare chauvinism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Welfare_chauvinism).[^cite_note-art-23]

In comparing the Western European and [post-Communist](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Post-Communist) Central European far-right, Kopeček writes that "\[t]he Central European far right was also typified by a strong anti-Communism, much more markedly than in Western Europe", allowing for "a basic ideological classification within a unified party family, despite the heterogeneity of the far right parties". Kopeček concludes that a comparison of Central European far-right parties with those of Western Europe shows that "these four elements are present in Central Europe as well, though in a somewhat modified form, despite differing political, economic, and social influences."[^cite_note-Kopeček_2007-19] In the American and more general Anglo-Saxon environment, the most common term is "[radical right](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Radical_right_\(United_States\))", which has a broader meaning than the [European radical right](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Radical_right_\(Europe\)).[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEMudde200213-24][^cite_note-Kopeček_2007-19] Mudde defines the American radical right as an "old school of nativism, populism, and hostility to central government \[which] was said to have developed into the post-World War II combination of ultranationalism and anti-communism, Christian fundamentalism, militaristic orientation, and anti-alien sentiment".[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEMudde200213-24]

[Jodi Dean](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jodi_Dean) argues that "the rise of far-right anti-communism in many parts of the world" should be interpreted "as a politics of fear, which utilizes the disaffection and anger generated by capitalism. \[...] Partisans of far right-wing organizations, in turn, use anti-communism to challenge every political current which is not embedded in a clearly exposed nationalist and racist agenda. For them, both the USSR and the European Union, leftist liberals, ecologists, and supranational corporations – all of these may be called 'communist' for the sake of their expediency."[^cite_note-Kuligowski,_Moll_&_Szadkowski_2019-25]

In *Hate in the Homeland: The New Global Far Right*, [Cynthia Miller-Idriss](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cynthia_Miller-Idriss) examines the far right as a global movement and representing a cluster of overlapping "[antidemocratic](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Criticism_of_democracy), antiegalitarian, [white supremacist](33178.md)" beliefs that are "embedded in solutions like [authoritarianism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Authoritarianism), [ethnic cleansing](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ethnic_cleansing) or ethnic migration, and the establishment of [separate ethno-states or enclaves](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/White_ethnostate) along racial and ethnic lines".[^cite_note-26]

## Modern debates

### Terminology



[Benito Mussolini](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Benito_Mussolini), dictator of [Fascist Italy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fascist_Italy) (left), and [Adolf Hitler](2731583.md), dictator of [Nazi Germany](21212.md) (right), were fascist leaders.

According to [Jean-Yves Camus](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jean-Yves_Camus) and [Nicolas Lebourg](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nicolas_Lebourg), the modern ambiguities in the definition of far-right politics lie in the fact that the concept is generally used by political adversaries to "disqualify and stigmatize all forms of partisan nationalism by reducing them to the historical experiments of [Italian Fascism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Italian_Fascism) \[and] [German National Socialism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/German_National_Socialism)".[^cite_note-FOOTNOTECamusLebourg20171–2-27] Mudde agrees and notes that "the term is not only used for scientific purposes but also for political purposes. Several authors define right-wing extremism as a sort of anti-thesis against their own beliefs."[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEMudde200210-28] While the existence of such a political position is widely accepted among scholars, figures associated with the far-right rarely accept this denomination, preferring terms like "national movement" or "national right".[^cite_note-FOOTNOTECamusLebourg20171–2-27] There is also debate about how appropriate the labels [neo-fascist](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Neo-fascist) or [neo-Nazi](54361.md) are. In the words of Mudde, "the labels Neo-Nazi and to a lesser extent neo-Fascism are now used exclusively for parties and groups that explicitly state a desire to restore the [Third Reich](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Third_Reich) or quote historical National Socialism as their ideological influence."[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEMudde200212-29]

According to Léonie de Jonge the term "far right", as used generally among [political scientists](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Political_science), is an [umbrella term](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Umbrella_term) which encompasses the theoretically distinct "radical right" and "extreme right".[^cite_note-30] One issue is whether parties should be labelled radical or extreme, a distinction that is made by the [Federal Constitutional Court of Germany](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Federal_Constitutional_Court_of_Germany) when determining whether or not a party should be banned.[^cite_note-31] Within the broader family of the far right, the extreme right is revolutionary, opposing popular sovereignty and majority rule, and sometimes supporting violence, whereas the radical right is reformist, accepting free elections, but opposing fundamental elements of liberal democracy such as minority rights, rule of law, or separation of powers.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEMudde201912-32]

After a survey of the academic literature, Mudde concluded in 2002 that the terms "right-wing extremism", "[right-wing populism](8354382.md)", "national populism", or "neo-populism" were often used as synonyms by scholars (or, nonetheless, terms with "striking similarities"), except notably among a few authors studying the extremist-theoretical tradition.[^cite_note-33]

### Relation to right-wing politics



Election poster of the far-right [Alternative for Germany](38708229.md) with a statement saying "Start [remigration](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Remigration) immediately!" in the [2024 Thuringian state election](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2024_Thuringian_state_election)

Italian philosopher and political scientist [Norberto Bobbio](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Norberto_Bobbio) argues that attitudes towards equality are primarily what distinguish [left-wing politics](18499.md) from [right-wing politics](56522.md) on the [political spectrum](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Political_spectrum):[^cite_note-34] "the left considers the key inequalities between people to be artificial and negative, which should be overcome by an active state, whereas the right believes that inequalities between people are natural and positive, and should be either defended or left alone by the state."[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEMudde201911-35]

Aspects of far-right ideology can be identified in the agenda of some contemporary right-wing parties: in particular, the idea that superior persons should dominate society while undesirable elements should be purged, which in extreme cases has resulted in [genocides](12441.md).[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEWoshinsky2008156-36] Charles Grant, director of the [Centre for European Reform](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Centre_for_European_Reform) in London, distinguishes between [fascism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fascism) and right-wing nationalist parties which are often described as far right such as the [National Front](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/National_Front_\(France\)) in France.[^cite_note-Baker-2016-37] Mudde notes that the most successful European far-right parties in 2019 were "former mainstream right-wing parties that have turned into populist radical right ones".[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEMudde2019\[\[Category\:Wikipedia_articles_needing_page_number_citations_from_August_2021\]\]<sup_class="noprint_Inline-Template_"_style="white-space\:nowrap;">\[<i>\[\[Wikipedia\:Citing_sources|<span_title="This_citation_requires_a_reference_to_the_specific_page_or_range_of_pages_in_which_the_material_appears. (August_2021)">page needed</span>\]\]</i>\]</sup>-38] According to historian [Mark Sedgwick](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mark_Sedgwick), "\[t]here is no general agreement as to where the mainstream ends and the extreme starts, and if there ever had been agreement on this, the recent shift in the mainstream would challenge it."[^cite_note-FOOTNOTESedgwick2019xiii-39]

Proponents of the [horseshoe theory](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Horseshoe_theory) interpretation of the [left–right political spectrum](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Left%E2%80%93right_political_spectrum) identify the [far left](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Far_left) and the far right as having more in common with each other as [extremists](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Extremists) than each of them has with [centrists](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Centrists) or [moderates](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Moderate).[^cite_note-40] This theory has received criticism,[^cite_note-Berlet_&_Lyons_2000,_p._342-41][^cite_note-42][^cite_note-43] including the argument that it has been centrists who have supported far-right and fascist regimes over socialist ones.[^cite_note-44]

### Nature of support

[Jens Rydgren](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jens_Rydgren) describes a number of theories as to why individuals support far-right political parties and the academic literature on this topic distinguishes between demand-side theories that have changed the "interests, emotions, attitudes and preferences of voters" and supply-side theories which focus on the programmes of parties, their organization and the opportunity structures within individual political systems.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTERydgren2007241–263-45] The most common demand-side theories are the [social breakdown thesis](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Social_breakdown_thesis), the [relative deprivation thesis](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Relative_deprivation_thesis), the [modernization losers thesis](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Modernization_losers_thesis) and the [ethnic competition thesis](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ethnic_competition_thesis).[^cite_note-FOOTNOTERydgren2007247-46]

The rise of far-right parties has also been viewed as a rejection of [post-materialist](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Post-materialism) values on the part of some voters. This theory which is known as the [reverse post-material thesis](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Reverse_post-material_thesis) blames both left-wing and [progressive](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Progressivism) parties for embracing a post-material agenda (including [feminism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Feminism) and [environmentalism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Environmentalism)) that alienates traditional working class voters.[^cite_note-47][^cite_note-48] Another study argues that individuals who join far-right parties determine whether those parties develop into major political players or whether they remain marginalized.[^cite_note-49]

Early academic studies adopted psychoanalytical explanations for the far right's support. The 1933 publication *[The Mass Psychology of Fascism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/The_Mass_Psychology_of_Fascism)* by [Wilhelm Reich](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Wilhelm_Reich) argued the theory that fascists came to power in Germany as a result of [sexual repression](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sexual_repression). For some far-right parties in Western Europe, the issue of immigration has become the dominant issue among them, so much so that some scholars refer to these parties as "anti-immigrant" parties.[^cite_note-50]

## Intellectual history

### Background

The [French Revolution](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/French_Revolution) in 1789 created a major shift in political thought by challenging the established ideas supporting hierarchy with new ones about universal [equality](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Social_equality) and [freedom](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Freedom).[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEBeiner201811-51] The modern [left–right political spectrum](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Left%E2%80%93right_political_spectrum) also emerged during this period. Democrats and proponents of [universal suffrage](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Universal_suffrage) were located on the left side of the elected French Assembly, while monarchists seated farthest to the right.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTECamusLebourg20171–2-27]

The strongest opponents of [liberalism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Liberalism) and [democracy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Democracy) during the 19th century, such as [Joseph de Maistre](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Joseph_de_Maistre) and [Friedrich Nietzsche](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Friedrich_Nietzsche), were highly critical of the French Revolution.[^cite_note-52] Those who advocated a return to the [absolute monarchy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Absolute_monarchy) during the 19th century called themselves "ultra-monarchists" and embraced a "[mystic](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mysticism)" and "[providentialist](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Providentialism)" vision of the world where royal dynasties were seen as the "repositories of divine will". The opposition to liberal modernity was based on the belief that hierarchy and rootedness are more important than equality and liberty, with the latter two being dehumanizing.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEBeiner201814-53]

### Emergence



[Charles Maurras](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Charles_Maurras), founder and leader of *[Action Française](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Action_Fran%C3%A7aise)*, a far-right monarchist and ultranationalist political movement in France

In the French public debate following the [Bolshevik Revolution](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bolshevik_Revolution) of 1917, *far right* was used to describe the strongest opponents of the *[far left](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Far_left)*, those who supported the events occurring in Russia.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTECamusLebourg201721-13] A number of thinkers on the far right nonetheless claimed an influence from an [anti-Marxist](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Anti-Marxist) and [anti-egalitarian](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Anti-egalitarian) interpretation of [socialism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Socialism), based on a military comradeship that rejected [Marxist](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Marxist) [class analysis](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Class_analysis), or what [Oswald Spengler](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Oswald_Spengler) had called a "socialism of the blood", which is sometimes described by scholars as a form of "[socialist revisionism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Revisionism_\(Marxism\))".[^cite_note-Vandenhoeck-2011-54] They included [Charles Maurras](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Charles_Maurras), [Benito Mussolini](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Benito_Mussolini), [Arthur Moeller van den Bruck](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Arthur_Moeller_van_den_Bruck) and [Ernst Niekisch](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ernst_Niekisch).[^cite_note-woods-55][^cite_note-François-2009-56][^cite_note-FOOTNOTECamusLebourg20177–8-57] Those thinkers eventually split along nationalist lines from the original [communist movement](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Communist_movement), [Karl Marx](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Karl_Marx) and [Friedrich Engels](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Friedrich_Engels) contradicting [nationalist](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nationalist) theories with the idea that "the working men \[had] no country."[^cite_note-FOOTNOTECamusLebourg20179–10-58] The main reason for that ideological confusion can be found in the consequences of the [Franco-Prussian War](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Franco-Prussian_War) of 1870, which according to Swiss historian [Philippe Burrin](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Philippe_Burrin) had completely redesigned the political landscape in Europe by diffusing the idea of an anti-individualistic concept of "national unity" rising above the right and left division.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTECamusLebourg20177–8-57]

As the concept of "the masses" was introduced into the political debate through [industrialization](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Industrialization) and the [universal suffrage](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Universal_suffrage), a new right-wing founded on national and social ideas began to emerge, what [Zeev Sternhell](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Zeev_Sternhell) has called the "revolutionary right" and a foreshadowing of [fascism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fascism). The rift between the left and nationalists was furthermore accentuated by the emergence of anti-militarist and anti-patriotic movements like [anarchism](12.md) or [syndicalism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Syndicalism), which shared even fewer similarities with the far right.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTECamusLebourg20179–10-58] The latter began to develop a "nationalist mysticism" entirely different from that on the left, and antisemitism turned into a credo of the far right, marking a break from the traditional economic "anti-Judaism" defended by parts of the far left, in favor of a racial and pseudo-scientific notion of [alterity](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Alterity). Various nationalist leagues began to form across Europe like the [Pan-German League](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pan-German_League) or the [Ligue des Patriotes](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ligue_des_Patriotes), with the common goal of a uniting the masses beyond social divisions.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTECamusLebourg201711–12-59][^cite_note-Dupeux-1994-60]

### *Völkisch* and revolutionary right



Spanish [Falangist](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Falangist) volunteer forces of the [Blue Division](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Blue_Division) entrain at [San Sebastián](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/San_Sebasti%C3%A1n), 1942

The [*Völkisch* movement](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/V%C3%B6lkisch_movement) emerged in the late 19th century, drawing inspiration from [German Romanticism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/German_Romanticism) and its fascination for a [medieval Reich](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Holy_Roman_Empire) supposedly organized into a harmonious hierarchical order. Erected on the idea of "[blood and soil](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Blood_and_soil)", it was a [racialist](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Racialism), [populist](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Populism), [agrarian](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Agrarianism), [romantic nationalist](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Romantic_nationalism) and an [antisemitic](1078.md) movement from the 1900s onward as a consequence of a growing exclusive and racial connotation.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTECamusLebourg201716–18-61] They idealized the myth of an "original nation", that still could be found at their times in the rural regions of Germany, a form of "primitive democracy freely subjected to their natural elites".[^cite_note-François-2009-56] Thinkers led by [Arthur de Gobineau](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Arthur_de_Gobineau), [Houston Stewart Chamberlain](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Houston_Stewart_Chamberlain), [Alexis Carrel](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Alexis_Carrel) and [Georges Vacher de Lapouge](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Georges_Vacher_de_Lapouge) distorted [Darwin](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Charles_Darwin)'s [theory of evolution](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Evolution) to advocate a "race struggle" and an hygienist vision of the world. The purity of the bio-mystical and primordial nation theorized by the *Völkischen* then began to be seen as corrupted by foreign elements, Jewish in particular.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTECamusLebourg201716–18-61]

Translated in [Maurice Barrès](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Maurice_Barr%C3%A8s)' concept of "the earth and the dead", these ideas influenced the pre-fascist "revolutionary right" across Europe. The latter had its origin in the *[fin de siècle](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fin_de_si%C3%A8cle)* intellectual crisis and it was, in the words of [Fritz Stern](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fritz_Stern), the deep "cultural despair" of thinkers feeling uprooted within the [rationalism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Rationalism) and [scientism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Scientism) of the modern world.[^cite_note-Stern-1974-62] It was characterized by a rejection of the established social order, with revolutionary tendencies and anti-capitalist stances, a populist and [plebiscitary](51582.md) dimension, the advocacy of violence as a means of action and a call for individual and collective [palingenesis](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Palingenesis) ("regeneration, rebirth").[^cite_note-FOOTNOTECamusLebourg201719-63]

### Contemporary thought

&#x20;The key thinkers of contemporary far-right politics are claimed by [Mark Sedgwick](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mark_Sedgwick) to share four key elements, namely [apocalyptism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Declinism), fear of [global elites](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Global_elite), belief in [Carl Schmitt](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Carl_Schmitt)'s [friend–enemy distinction](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/The_Concept_of_the_Political) and the idea of [metapolitics](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Metapolitics).[^cite_note-FOOTNOTESedgwick2019-64] The apocalyptic strain of thought begins in [Oswald Spengler](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Oswald_Spengler)'s *[The Decline of the West](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/The_Decline_of_the_West)* and is shared by [Julius Evola](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Julius_Evola) and [Alain de Benoist](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Alain_de_Benoist). It continues in *[The Death of the West](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/The_Death_of_the_West)* by [Pat Buchanan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pat_Buchanan) as well as in fears over [Islamization of Europe](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Eurabia).[^cite_note-FOOTNOTESedgwick2019-64] [Ernst Jünger](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ernst_J%C3%BCnger) was concerned about [rootless cosmopolitan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Rootless_cosmopolitan) elites while de Benoist and Buchanan oppose the [managerial state](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Managerial_state) and [Curtis Yarvin](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Curtis_Yarvin) is against "the Cathedral".[^cite_note-FOOTNOTESedgwick2019-64] Schmitt's friend–enemy distinction has inspired the French [Nouvelle Droite](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nouvelle_Droite) idea of [ethnopluralism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ethnopluralism).[^cite_note-FOOTNOTESedgwick2019-64]



[CasaPound](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/CasaPound) rally in Naples

In a 1961 book deemed influential in the European far-right at large, French neo-fascist writer [Maurice Bardèche](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Maurice_Bard%C3%A8che) introduced the idea that fascism could survive the 20th century under a new [metapolitical](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Metapolitics) guise adapted to the changes of the times. Rather than trying to revive doomed regimes with their [single party](102459.md), [secret police](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Secret_police) or public display of [Caesarism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Caesarism), Bardèche argued that its theorists should promote the core philosophical idea of [fascism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fascism) regardless of its framework,[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEBar-On2016xiii-14] i.e. the concept that only a minority, "the physically saner, the morally purer, the most conscious of national interest", can represent best the community and serve the less gifted in what Bardèche calls a new "[feudal](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Feudalism) contract".[^cite_note-65]

Another influence on contemporary far-right thought has been the [Traditionalist School](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Traditionalist_School), which included [Julius Evola](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Julius_Evola), and has influenced [Steve Bannon](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Steve_Bannon) and [Aleksandr Dugin](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Aleksandr_Dugin), advisors to [Donald Trump](4848272.md) and [Vladimir Putin](32817.md) as well as the [Jobbik](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jobbik) party in Hungary.[^cite_note-66]

## International organizations



National origins of Fascist International Congress participants in 1934

During the rise of Nazi Germany, far-right international organizations began to emerge in the 1930s with the [International Conference of Fascist Parties](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/International_Conference_of_Fascist_Parties?action=edit\&redlink=1) in 1932 and the [Fascist International Congress](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fascist_International_Congress) in 1934.[^cite_note-OUP-2018-67] During the 1934 Fascist International Conference, the *[Comitati d'Azione per l'Universalità di Roma](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Comitati_d'Azione_per_l'Universalit%C3%A0_di_Roma?action=edit\&redlink=1)* (CAUR; English: Action Committees for the Universality of Rome), created by [Benito Mussolini](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Benito_Mussolini)'s [Fascist Regime](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Italian_fascism) to create a network for a "Fascist International", representatives from far-right groups gathered in [Montreux](51548.md), Switzerland, including [Romania](25445.md)'s [Iron Guard](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Iron_Guard), [Norway](21241.md)'s [Nasjonal Samling](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nasjonal_Samling), the [Greek National Socialist Party](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Greek_National_Socialist_Party), [Spain](26667.md)'s [Falange](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Falange_Espa%C3%B1ola_y_de_las_JONS) movement, [Ireland's](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Irish_Free_State) [Blueshirts](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Blueshirts), [France](5843419.md)'s *[Mouvement Franciste](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mouvement_Franciste) and* [Portugal](23033.md)'s [União Nacional](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Uni%C3%A3o_Nacional), among others.[^cite_note-68][^cite_note-Stanley_G_page_105-69] However, no international group was fully established before the outbreak of [World War II](32927.md).[^cite_note-OUP-2018-67]

Following World War II, other far-right organizations attempted to establish themselves, such as the European organizations of [Nouvel Ordre Européen](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nouvel_Ordre_Europ%C3%A9en), [European Social Movement](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/European_Social_Movement) and [Circulo Español de Amigos de Europa](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Circulo_Espa%C3%B1ol_de_Amigos_de_Europa) or the further-reaching [World Union of National Socialists](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/World_Union_of_National_Socialists) and the [League for Pan-Nordic Friendship](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/League_for_Pan-Nordic_Friendship?action=edit\&redlink=1).[^cite_note-TUC-2020-70] Beginning in the 1980s, far-right groups began to solidify themselves through official political avenues.[^cite_note-TUC-2020-70]

With the founding of the [European Union](9317.md) in 1993, far-right groups began to espouse [Euroscepticism](10037.md), nationalist and anti-migrant beliefs.[^cite_note-OUP-2018-67] By 2010, the Eurosceptic group [European Alliance for Freedom](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/European_Alliance_for_Freedom) emerged and saw some prominence during the [2014 European Parliament election](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2014_European_Parliament_election).[^cite_note-OUP-2018-67][^cite_note-TUC-2020-70] The majority of far-right groups in the 2010s began to establish international contacts with right-wing coalitions to develop a solidified platform.[^cite_note-OUP-2018-67] In 2017, [Steve Bannon](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Steve_Bannon) would create [The Movement](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/The_Movement_\(right-wing_populist_group\)), an organization to create an international far-right group based on [Aleksandr Dugin](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Aleksandr_Dugin)'s *[The Fourth Political Theory](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/The_Fourth_Political_Theory)*, for the [2019 European Parliament election](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2019_European_Parliament_election).[^cite_note-71][^cite_note-Jacobin-2019-72] The European Alliance for Freedom would also reorganize into [Identity and Democracy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Identity_and_Democracy) for the 2019 European Parliament election.[^cite_note-TUC-2020-70] The [Euronat](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Euronat) (1997–2009), [Alliance of European National Movements](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Alliance_of_European_National_Movements) (2009–2019), [Alliance for Peace and Freedom](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Alliance_for_Peace_and_Freedom) are far-right European political alliances in the EU.

The far-right Spanish party [Vox](41928488.md) initially introduced the [Madrid Charter](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Madrid_Charter) project, a planned group to denounce left-wing groups in [Ibero-America](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ibero-America), to the government of United States president [Donald Trump](4848272.md) while visiting the [United States](3434750.md) in February 2019, with [Santiago Abascal](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Santiago_Abascal) and [Rafael Bardají](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Rafael_Bardaj%C3%AD) using their good relations with the administration to build support within the [Republican Party](32070.md) and establishing strong ties with American contacts.[^cite_note-Jacobin-2019-72][^cite_note-Carvajal-2020-73][^cite_note-González-2021-74] In March 2019, Abascal tweeted an image of himself wearing a [morion](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Morion_\(helmet\)) similar to a [conquistador](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Conquistador), with *[ABC](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/ABC_\(newspaper\))* writing in an article detailing the document that this event provided a narrative that "symbolizes in part the [expansionist](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Expansionist) mood of Vox and its ideology far from Spain".[^cite_note-ABC-2021-75] The charter subsequently grew to include signers that had little to no relation to Latin America and Spanish-speaking areas.[^cite_note-Mitralias-2022-76] Vox has advised [Javier Milei](56599840.md) in [Argentina](18951905.md), the [Bolsonaro family](46603496.md) in [Brazil](3383.md), [José Antonio Kast](55052190.md) in [Chile](5489.md) and [Keiko Fujimori](4643445.md) in [Peru](170691.md).[^cite_note-77]

Nationalists from Europe and the United States met at a Holiday Inn in St. Petersburg on March 22, 2015, for first convention of the International Russian Conservative Forum organized by pro-Putin [Rodina](3556828.md)-party. The event was attended by fringe right-wing extremists like [Nordic Resistance Movement](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nordic_Resistance_Movement) from Scandinavia but also by more mainstream [MEPs](258909.md) from [Golden Dawn](1938004.md) and [National Democratic Party of Germany](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/National_Democratic_Party_of_Germany). In addition to Rodina, Russian neo-Nazis from [Russian Imperial Movement](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Russian_Imperial_Movement) and [Rusich Group](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Rusich_Group) were also in attendance. The event was attended by several notable American white supremacists including [Jared Taylor](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jared_Taylor) and [Brandon Russell](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Brandon_Russell).[^cite_note-forum-78]

### Africa

#### Morocco

Morocco saw a spread of [ultranationalism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ultranationalism), [antifeminism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Antifeminism), and [opposition to immigration](6491626.md) themes in digital spaces.[^cite_note-s175-79]

#### Rwanda

A number of far-right extremist and paramilitary groups carried out the [Rwandan genocide](676659.md) under the [racial supremacist](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Racial_supremacist) ideology of [Hutu Power](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hutu_Power), developed by journalist and Hutu supremacist [Hassan Ngeze](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hassan_Ngeze).[^cite_note-Saha-80] On 5 July 1975, exactly two years after the [1973 Rwandan coup d'état](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1973_Rwandan_coup_d'%C3%A9tat), the far right [National Republican Movement for Democracy and Development](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/National_Republican_Movement_for_Democracy_and_Development) (MRND) was founded under president [Juvénal Habyarimana](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Juv%C3%A9nal_Habyarimana). Between 1975 and 1991, the MRND was the only legal political party in the country. It was dominated by [Hutus](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hutu), particularly from Habyarimana's home region of Northern Rwanda. An elite group of MRND party members who were known to have influence on the President and his wife [Agathe Habyarimana](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Agathe_Habyarimana) are known as the *[akazu](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Akazu)*, an informal organization of Hutu extremists whose members planned and led the 1994 Rwandan genocide.[^cite_note-81][^cite_note-HRW-82] [Félicien Kabuga](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/F%C3%A9licien_Kabuga), a prominent Hutu businessman and member of the *akazu*, was a leading financier of the genocide; he provided thousands of machetes, which were the usual weapons used to kill.[^cite_note-83] Kabuga also founded [Radio Télévision Libre des Mille Collines](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Radio_T%C3%A9l%C3%A9vision_Libre_des_Mille_Collines), used to broadcast propaganda and direct the [génocidaires](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/G%C3%A9nocidaires). Kabuga was arrested in France on 16 May 2020, and charged with [crimes against humanity](61099.md).[^cite_note-84]

##### Interahamwe

Main article: [Interahamwe](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Interahamwe)

The Interahamwe was formed around 1990 as the [youth wing](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Youth_wing) of the MRND and enjoyed the backing of the Hutu Power government. The Interahamwe were driven out of Rwanda after [Tutsi](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tutsi)-led [Rwandan Patriotic Front](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Rwandan_Patriotic_Front) victory in the Rwandan Civil War in July 1994 and are considered a [terrorist organization](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Terrorist_organisation) by many African and Western governments. The Interahamwe and splinter groups such as the [Democratic Forces for the Liberation of Rwanda](1676875.md) continue to wage an [insurgency](589841.md) against Rwanda from neighboring countries, where they are also involved in local conflicts and terrorism. The Interahamwe were the main perpetrators of the Rwandan genocide, during which an estimated 500,000 to 1,000,000 Tutsi, [Twa](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Twa) and moderate Hutus were killed from April to July 1994 and the term *Interahamwe* was widened to mean any civilian bands killing Tutsi.[^cite_note-85][^cite_note-86]

##### Coalition for the Defence of the Republic

Main article: [Coalition for the Defence of the Republic](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Coalition_for_the_Defence_of_the_Republic)

Other far-right groups and paramilitaries involved included the [anti-democratic](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Anti-democratic) [segregationist](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Segregationist) Coalition for the Defence of the Republic (CDR), which called for complete segregation of Hutus from Tutsis. The CDR had a paramilitary wing known as the [Impuzamugambi](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Impuzamugambi). Together with the Interahamwe militia, the Impuzamugambi played a central role in the Rwandan genocide.[^cite_note-87][^cite_note-Saha-80]

#### South Africa

##### Herstigte Nasionale Party

Main article: [Herstigte Nasionale Party](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Herstigte_Nasionale_Party)

In 1969, the Herstigte Nasionale Party (HNP) under [Albert Hertzog](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Albert_Hertzog) emerged as a breakaway group from the governing South African [National Party](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/National_Party_\(South_Africa\)), an [Afrikaner](18947965.md) [ethno-nationalist](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ethno-nationalist) party that implemented the racist, segregationist program of [apartheid](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Apartheid), the legal system of political, economic and social separation of the races intended to maintain and extend political and economic control of South Africa by the White minority.[^cite_note-88][^cite_note-89][^cite_note-90] The HNP was formed after the South African National Party re-established diplomatic relations with [Malawi](19088.md) and legislated to allow [Māori](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/M%C4%81ori_people) players and spectators to enter the country during the 1970 [New Zealand rugby union team](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/New_Zealand_rugby_union_team) tour in South Africa.[^cite_note-91] The HNP advocated for a [Calvinist](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Calvinist), racially segregated and [Afrikaans](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Afrikaans)-speaking nation.[^cite_note-92]

##### Afrikaner Weerstandsbeweging

Main article: [Afrikaner Weerstandsbeweging](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Afrikaner_Weerstandsbeweging)

In 1973, [Eugène Terre'Blanche](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Eug%C3%A8ne_Terre'Blanche), a former [police officer](3281856.md) founded the Afrikaner Weerstandsbeweging (Afrikaner Resistance Movement), a South African [neo-Nazi](54361.md) paramilitary organization, often described as a [white supremacist](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/White_supremacist) group.[^cite_note-93][^cite_note-94][^cite_note-95] Since its founding in 1973 by Eugène Terre'Blanche and six other far-right Afrikaners, it has been dedicated to [secessionist](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Secessionist) Afrikaner nationalism and the creation of an independent [Boer](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Boer)-Afrikaner republic in part of South Africa. During [negotiations to end apartheid in South Africa](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Negotiations_to_end_apartheid_in_South_Africa) in the early 1990s, the organization terrorized and killed black South Africans.[^cite_note-96]

#### Togo

Main article: [Human rights in Togo](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Human_rights_in_Togo)

Togo has been ruled by members of the Gnassingbé family and the far-right [military dictatorship](146728.md) formerly known as the [Rally of the Togolese People](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Rally_of_the_Togolese_People) since 1969. Despite the legalization of political parties in 1991 and the ratification of a democratic constitution in 1992, the regime continues to be regarded as oppressive. In 1993, the European Union cut off aid in reaction to the regime's human-rights offenses. After's Eyadema's death in 2005, his son Faure Gnassingbe took over, then stood down and was re-elected in elections that were widely described as fraudulent and occasioned violence that resulted in as many as 600 deaths and the flight from Togo of 40,000 refugees.[^cite_note-97] In 2012, Faure Gnassingbe dissolved the RTP and created the [Union for the Republic](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Union_for_the_Republic_\(Togo\)).[^cite_note-98][^cite_note-99][^cite_note-100]

Throughout the reign of the Gnassingbé family, Togo has been extremely oppressive. According to a [United States Department of State](31975.md) report based on conditions in 2010, human rights abuses are common and include "security force use of excessive force, including [torture](47702.md), which resulted in deaths and injuries; official impunity; harsh and life-threatening prison conditions; arbitrary arrests and detention; lengthy pretrial detention; executive influence over the judiciary; infringement of citizens' privacy rights; restrictions on [freedoms of press](105323.md), [assembly](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Freedom_of_Assembly), and movement; official corruption; discrimination and violence against women; child abuse, including female genital mutilation (FGM), and sexual exploitation of children; regional and ethnic discrimination; trafficking in persons, especially women and children; societal discrimination against persons with disabilities; official and societal discrimination against homosexual persons; societal discrimination against persons with [HIV](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Human_immunodeficiency_virus); and forced labor, including by children."[^cite_note-101]

### Americas

See also: [Fascism in North America](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fascism_in_North_America) and [Fascism in South America](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fascism_in_South_America)

#### Brazil

See also: [Bolsonarism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bolsonarism)



Children make the [Nazi salute](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nazi_salute) in [Presidente Bernardes](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Presidente_Bernardes,_S%C3%A3o_Paulo), Brazil, circa 1935.

During the 1920s and 1930s, a local brand of religious fascism appeared known as [Brazilian Integralism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Brazilian_Integralism), coalescing around the party known as [Brazilian Integralist Action](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Brazilian_Integralist_Action). It adopted many characteristics of European fascist movements, including a green-shirted [paramilitary](146730.md) organization with [uniformed](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Uniform) ranks, highly regimented street demonstrations and rhetoric against [Marxism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Marxism) and [liberalism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Liberalism).[^cite_note-102]

Prior to World War II, the Nazi Party had been making and distributing propaganda among ethnic Germans in Brazil. The Nazi regime built close ties with Brazil through the estimated 100 thousand native Germans and 1 million German descendants living in Brazil at the time.[^cite_note-103] In 1928, the Brazilian section of the Nazi Party was founded in Timbó, Santa Catarina. This section reached 2,822 members and was the largest section of the Nazi Party outside Germany.[^cite_note-104][^cite_note-105] About 100 thousand born Germans and about one million descendants lived in Brazil at that time.[^cite_note-106]

After Germany's defeat in World War II, many Nazi war criminals fled to Brazil and hid among the German-Brazilian communities. The most notable example of this was [Josef Mengele](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Josef_Mengele), a Nazi SS officer and physician known as the "Angel of Death" for his deadly experiments on prisoners at the [Auschwitz II (Birkenau)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Auschwitz_II_\(Birkenau\)) concentration camp, who fled first to Argentina, then Paraguay, before finally settling in Brazil in 1960. Mengele eventually drowned in 1979 in [Bertioga](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bertioga), on the coast of São Paulo state, without ever having been recognized in his 19 years in Brazil.[^cite_note-107]

The far right has continued to operate throughout Brazil[^cite_note-108] and a number of far-right parties existed in the modern era including [Patriota](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Patriota), the [Brazilian Labour Renewal Party](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Brazilian_Labour_Renewal_Party), the [Party of the Reconstruction of the National Order](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Party_of_the_Reconstruction_of_the_National_Order), the [National Renewal Alliance](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/National_Renewal_Alliance) and the [Social Liberal Party](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Social_Liberal_Party_\(Brazil\)) as well as [death squads](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Death_squads) such as the [Command for Hunting Communists](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Command_for_Hunting_Communists). Former [President of Brazil](273638.md) [Jair Bolsonaro](46603496.md) was a member of the [Alliance for Brazil](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Alliance_for_Brazil), a far-right nationalist political group that aimed to become a political party, until 2022, when the party was disbanded. Since 2022, he is a member of the [Liberal Party](9373363.md).[^cite_note-109][^cite_note-110][^cite_note-111] Bolsonaro has been widely described by numerous media organizations as far right.[^cite_note-112]

#### Canada

Main article: [Far-right politics in Canada](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Far-right_politics_in_Canada_\(disambiguation\))

#### Guatemala

Main article: [National Liberation Movement (Guatemala)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/National_Liberation_Movement_\(Guatemala\))

In Guatemala, the far-right[^cite_note-Forsythe_2009-113][^cite_note-Bartrop_2014-114] government of [Carlos Castillo Armas](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Carlos_Castillo_Armas) utilized [death squads](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Death_squad) after coming to power in the [1954 Guatemalan coup d'état](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1954_Guatemalan_coup_d'%C3%A9tat).[^cite_note-Forsythe_2009-113][^cite_note-Bartrop_2014-114] Along with other far-right extremists, Castillo Armas started the [National Liberation Movement](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/National_Liberation_Movement_\(Guatemala\)) (*Movimiento de Liberación Nacional*, or MLN). The founders of the party described it as the "party of organized violence".[^cite_note-Levenson-Estrada-115] The new government promptly reversed the democratic reforms initiated during the [Guatemalan Revolution](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Guatemalan_Revolution) and the [agrarian reform](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Agrarian_reform) program ([Decree 900](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Decree_900)) that was the main project of president [Jacobo Arbenz Guzman](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jacobo_Arbenz_Guzman) and which directly impacted the interests of both the [United Fruit Company](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/United_Fruit_Company) and the Guatemalan landowners.[^cite_note-116][^cite_note-117]

Mano Blanca, otherwise known as the Movement of Organized Nationalist Action, was set up in 1966 as a front for the MLN to carry out its more violent activities,[^cite_note-118][^cite_note-Grandin-119] along with many other similar groups, including the New Anticommunist Organization and the Anticommunist Council of Guatemala.[^cite_note-Levenson-Estrada-115][^cite_note-120] Mano Blanca was active during the governments of colonel [Carlos Arana Osorio](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Carlos_Arana_Osorio) and general [Kjell Laugerud García](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kjell_Laugerud_Garc%C3%ADa) and was dissolved by general [Fernando Romeo Lucas Garcia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fernando_Romeo_Lucas_Garcia) in 1978.[^cite_note-121]

Armed with the support and coordination of the Guatemalan Armed Forces, Mano Blanca began a campaign described by the United States Department of State as one of "kidnappings, torture, and [summary execution](1227309.md)".[^cite_note-Grandin-119] One of the main targets of Mano Blanca was the [Revolutionary Party](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Revolutionary_Party_\(Guatemala\)), an anti-communist group that was the only major reform oriented party allowed to operate under the military-dominated regime. Other targets included the banned leftist parties.[^cite_note-Grandin-119] Human rights activist [Blase Bonpane](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Blase_Bonpane) described the activities of Mano Blanca as being an integral part of the policy of the Guatemalan government and by extension the policy of the United States government and the [Central Intelligence Agency](5183633.md).[^cite_note-122] Overall, Mano Blanca was responsible for thousands of murders and kidnappings, leading travel writer [Paul Theroux](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Paul_Theroux) to refer to them as "Guatemala's version of a volunteer Gestapo unit".[^cite_note-123]

#### Chile



[Dictator of Chile](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Military_dictatorship_of_Chile_\(1973%E2%80%931990\)) [Augusto Pinochet](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Augusto_Pinochet) meeting with [United States President](24113.md) [George H. W. Bush](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/George_H._W._Bush) in 1990

The [National Socialist Movement of Chile](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/National_Socialist_Movement_of_Chile) (MNSCH) was created in the 1930s with the funding from the German population in Chile.[^cite_note-Payne-124] In 1938, the MNSCH was dissolved after [it attempted a coup](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Seguro_Obrero_massacre) and recreated itself as the [Popular Freedom Alliance](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Popular_Freedom_Alliance) party, later merging with the Agrarian Party to create the [Agrarian Labor Party](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Agrarian_Labor_Party) (PAL).[^cite_note-125] PAL would go through various mergers to become the [Partido Nacional Popular (Chile)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/National_People's_Party_\(Chile\)?action=edit\&redlink=1), then [National Action](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/National_Action_\(Chile,_1963\)) and finally the [National Party](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/National_Party_\(Chile,_1966\)).

Following the fall of Nazi Germany, many Nazis fled to Chile.[^cite_note-Levenda-2002-126] The National Party supported the [1973 Chilean coup d'état](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1973_Chilean_coup_d'%C3%A9tat) that established the [military dictatorship of](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Military_dictatorship_of_Chile_\(1973%E2%80%931990\)) [Augusto Pinochet](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Augusto_Pinochet) with many members assuming positions in Pinochet's government. Pinochet headed a far-right dictatorship in Chile from 1973 to 1990.[^cite_note-Leal-2020-127][^cite_note-128] According to author [Peter Levenda](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Peter_Levenda), Pinochet was "openly pro-Nazi" and used former [Gestapo](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Gestapo) members to train his own [Dirección de Inteligencia Nacional](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Direcci%C3%B3n_de_Inteligencia_Nacional) (DINA) personnel.[^cite_note-Levenda-2002-126] Pinochet's DINA sent political prisoners to the Chilean-German town of [Colonia Dignidad](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Colonia_Dignidad), with the town's actions being defended by the Pinochet government.[^cite_note-Levenda-2002-126][^cite_note-Falconer-2008-129][^cite_note-Infield-1981-130] The [Central Intelligence Agency](5183633.md) and [Simon Wiesenthal](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Simon_Wiesenthal) also provided evidence of [Josef Mengele](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Josef_Mengele) – the infamous Nazi [concentration camp](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Concentration_camp) doctor known as the "Angel of Death" for his lethal [experiments on human subjects](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nazi_human_experimentation) – being present in Colonia Dignidad.[^cite_note-Levenda-2002-126][^cite_note-Infield-1981-130] Former DINA member [Michael Townley](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Michael_Townley) also stated that [biological warfare](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Biological_warfare) weapons experiments occurred at the colony.[^cite_note-131]

Following the end of Pinochet's government, the National Party would split to become the more centrist [National Renewal](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/National_Renewal_\(Chile\)) (RN), while individuals who supported Pinochet organized [Independent Democratic Union](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Independent_Democratic_Union) (UDI). UDI is a far-right political party that was formed by former Pinochet officials.[^cite_note-132][^cite_note-133][^cite_note-134][^cite_note-135] In 2019, the far-right [Republican Party](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Republican_Party_\(Chile,_2019\)) was founded by [José Antonio Kast](55052190.md), a UDI politician who believed his former party criticized Pinochet too often.[^cite_note-URU-136][^cite_note-Funk-2021-137][^cite_note-138][^cite_note-139] According to Cox and Blanco, the Republican Party appeared in Chilean politics in a similar manner to Spain's [Vox](41928488.md) party, with both parties splitting off from an existing right wing party to collect disillusioned voters.[^cite_note-Dávila-2020-140]

#### El Salvador

Main article: [Death squads in El Salvador](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Death_squads_in_El_Salvador)



A billboard serving as a reminder of one of many [massacres in El Salvador](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_massacres_in_El_Salvador) that occurred during the civil war

During the [Salvadoran Civil War](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Salvadoran_Civil_War), far-right death squads known in Spanish by the name of *Escuadrón de la Muerte*, literally "Squadron of Death, achieved notoriety when a [sniper](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sniper) assassinated Archbishop [Óscar Romero](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/%C3%93scar_Romero) while he was saying [mass](5684685.md) in March 1980. In December 1980, [three American nuns and a lay worker](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1980_murders_of_U.S._missionaries_in_El_Salvador) were [gangraped](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Gangrape) and murdered by a military unit later found to have been acting on specific orders. Death squads were instrumental in killing thousands of peasants and activists. Funding for the squads came primarily from right-wing Salvadoran businessmen and landowners.[^cite_note-141]

El Salvadorian death squads indirectly received arms, funding, training and advice during the [Jimmy Carter](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Presidency_of_Jimmy_Carter), [Ronald Reagan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Presidency_of_Ronald_Reagan) and [George H. W. Bush](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Presidency_of_George_H._W._Bush) administrations.[^cite_note-142] Some death squads such as [Sombra Negra](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sombra_Negra) are still operating in El Salvador.[^cite_note-143]

#### Honduras

Main article: [Death squads in Honduras](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Death_squads_in_Honduras)

Honduras also had far-right death squads active through the 1980s, the most notorious of which was [Battalion 3–16](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Battalion_3-16_\(Honduras\)). Hundreds of people, teachers, politicians and union bosses were assassinated by government-backed forces. Battalion 316 received substantial support and training from the United States through the Central Intelligence Agency.[^cite_note-144] At least nineteen members were [School of the Americas](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Western_Hemisphere_Institute_for_Security_Cooperation) graduates.[^cite_note-republic_SOA_nonstop-145][^cite_note-derechos_316_soa-146] As of mid-2006, seven members, including [Billy Joya](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Billy_Joya), later played important roles in the administration of President [Manuel Zelaya](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Manuel_Zelaya).[^cite_note-mesoamerica_200606-147]

Following the [2009 Honduran constitutional crisis](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2009_Honduran_constitutional_crisis), former Battalion 3–16 member [Nelson Willy Mejía Mejía](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nelson_Willy_Mej%C3%ADa_Mej%C3%ADa) became Director-General of Immigration[^cite_note-NCR_NWMejiaMejia-148][^cite_note-cofadeh_NMWW-149] and Billy Joya was *de facto* President [Roberto Micheletti](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Roberto_Micheletti)'s security advisor.[^cite_note-DemocNow_Zelaya-150] [Napoleón Nassar Herrera](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Napole%C3%B3n_Nassar_Herrera), another former Battalion 3–16 member,[^cite_note-mesoamerica_200606-147][^cite_note-nizkor_nassar-151] was high Commissioner of Police for the north-west region under Zelaya and under Micheletti, even becoming a Secretary of Security spokesperson "for dialogue" under Micheletti.[^cite_note-elnuevo_nazar-152][^cite_note-mines_nassar-153] Zelaya claimed that Joya had reactivated the death squad, with dozens of government opponents having been murdered since the ascent of the Michiletti and Lobo governments.[^cite_note-DemocNow_Zelaya-150]

#### Mexico

##### National Synarchist Union

Main article: [National Synarchist Union](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/National_Synarchist_Union)

The largest far-right party in Mexico is the National Synarchist Union. It was historically a movement of the Roman Catholic extreme right, in some ways akin to [clerical fascism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Clerical_fascism) and [Falangism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Falangism), strongly opposed to the [left-wing](18499.md) and [secularist](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Secularist) policies of the [Institutional Revolutionary Party](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Institutional_Revolutionary_Party) and its predecessors that governed Mexico from 1929 to 2000 and 2012 to 2018.[^cite_note-154][^cite_note-155]

#### Peru

##### Fujimorism

Further information: [Fujimorism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fujimorism)



[Alberto Fujimori](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Alberto_Fujimori), the creator of [Fujimorism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fujimorism)

During the [internal conflict in Peru](9624647.md) and a struggling presidency of [Alan García](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Alan_Garc%C3%ADa), the [Peruvian Armed Forces](23438.md) created [Plan Verde](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Plan_Verde), initially a coup plan that involved establishing a government that would carry out the [genocide](12441.md) of impoverished and indigenous Peruvians, the control or [censorship](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Censorship) of media and the establishment of a [neoliberal](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Neoliberal) economy controlled by a [military junta](216815.md) in Peru.[^cite_note-Rospigliosi-1996b-156][^cite_note-CANbio-157][^cite_note-Burt-1998-158] Military planners also decided against the coup as they expected [Mario Vargas Llosa](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mario_Vargas_Llosa), a neoliberal candidate, to be elected in the [1990 Peruvian general election](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1990_Peruvian_general_election).[^cite_note-Avilés-2009-159][^cite_note-Rospigliosi-1996-160] Vargas Llosa later reported that [Anthony C. E. Quainton](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Anthony_C._E._Quainton), the [United States Ambassador to Peru](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/United_States_Ambassador_to_Peru), personally told him that allegedly leaked documents of the [Central Intelligence Agency](5183633.md) (CIA) purportedly being supportive of his opponent [Alberto Fujimori](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Alberto_Fujimori) were authentic, reportedly due to Fujimori's relationship with [Vladimiro Montesinos](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Vladimiro_Montesinos), a former [National Intelligence Service (SIN)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/National_Intelligence_Service_\(Peru\)) officer who was tasked with spying on the Peruvian military for the CIA.[^cite_note-REND12-161][^cite_note-Alfredo2-162] An agreement was ultimately adopted between the armed forces and Fujimori after he was inaugurated president,[^cite_note-Avilés-2009-159] with many of the objectives outlined in Plan Verde implemented by Fujimori and his followers.[^cite_note-Alfredo2-162][^cite_note-Avilés-2009-159] Fujimori then established [Fujimorism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fujimorism), an ideology with authoritarian[^cite_note-PROFE12-163] and conservative traits[^cite_note-164][^cite_note-165] that is still prevalent throughout Peru's institutions,[^cite_note-Villalba-2022-166] leading Peru through the [1992 Peruvian coup d'état](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1992_Peruvian_coup_d'%C3%A9tat) until he fled to [Japan](15573.md) in 2000 during the [Vladivideos](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Vladivideos) scandal. Following [Alberto Fujimori's arrest and trial](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Alberto_Fujimori's_arrest_and_trial), his daughter [Keiko Fujimori](4643445.md) assumed leadership of the Fujimorist movement and established [Popular Force](15247524.md), a far-right political party.[^cite_note-167][^cite_note-168][^cite_note-169] The [2016 Peruvian general election](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2016_Peruvian_general_election) resulted with the party holding the most power in the [Congress of Peru](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Congress_of_Peru) from 2016 to 2019, marking the beginning of a [political crisis](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2017%E2%80%93present_Peruvian_political_crisis). Following the [2021 Peruvian general election](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2021_Peruvian_general_election), far-right politician [Rafael López Aliaga](65483323.md) and his party [Popular Renewal](66197743.md) rose in popularity[^cite_note-170] and a far-right Congress – with the body's largest far-right bloc being Popular Force, Popular Renewal and [Advance Country](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Advance_Country)[^cite_note-FAR-171] – was elected into office.[^cite_note-172] Following the election, [La Resistencia Dios, Patria y Familia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/La_Resistencia_Dios,_Patria_y_Familia), a [neofascist](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Neofascist) militant organization would promote Fujimorism and oppose President [Pedro Castillo](49637220.md).[^cite_note-173][^cite_note-174][^cite_note-175]

#### United States

See also: [Fascism in the United States](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fascism_in_the_United_States)

In [United States politics](32021.md), the terms "extreme right", "far-right", and "ultra-right" are labels used to describe "militant forms of insurgent revolutionary right ideology and separatist ethnocentric nationalism", according to *[The Public Eye](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/The_Public_Eye_\(magazine\))*.[^cite_note-Political_Research_Assoc-2011-176] The terms are used for groups and movements such as [Christian Identity](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Christian_Identity),[^cite_note-Political_Research_Assoc-2011-176] the [Creativity Movement](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Creativity_\(religion\)),[^cite_note-Political_Research_Assoc-2011-176] the [Ku Klux Klan](16779.md),[^cite_note-Political_Research_Assoc-2011-176] the [National Socialist Movement](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/National_Socialist_Movement_\(United_States\)),[^cite_note-Political_Research_Assoc-2011-176][^cite_note-SPLCenter-177][^cite_note-ADL_2020-178] the [National Alliance](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/National_Alliance_\(United_States\)),[^cite_note-Political_Research_Assoc-2011-176] the [Joy of Satan Ministries](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Joy_of_Satan_Ministries),[^cite_note-SPLCenter-177][^cite_note-ADL_2020-178] and the [Order of Nine Angles](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Order_of_Nine_Angles).[^cite_note-Upchurch_2021-179] These far-right groups share [conspiracist views](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Conspiracy_theories) of power[^cite_note-180] which are often [antisemitic](1078.md)[^cite_note-181] and reject [pluralist democracy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pluralist_democracy) in favor of an [organic](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Organicism#In_politics_and_sociology) [oligarchy](22315.md)[^cite_note-182] that would unite the perceived homogeneously racial *[Völkish](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/V%C3%B6lkisch_movement)* nation.[^cite_note-Political_Research_Assoc-2011-176] The far-right in the United States is composed of various [neo-fascist](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Neo-fascism), [neo-Nazi](54361.md), [white nationalist](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/White_nationalism), and [white supremacist](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/White_supremacism) organizations and networks who have been known to refer to an "[acceleration](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Accelerationism#Far-right_accelerationist_terrorism)"[^cite_note-183] of racial conflict[^cite_note-\:1-184] through violent means such as [assassinations](2963.md), [murders](20171.md), [terrorist attacks](5210960.md),[^cite_note-185] and [societal collapse](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Societal_collapse),[^cite_note-\:1-184] in order to achieve the building of a [white ethnostate](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/White_ethnostate).[^cite_note-186][^cite_note-187]

##### Radical right

Main article: [Radical right (United States)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Radical_right_\(United_States\))



Though US President Trump said in September 2025 that "the radicals on the left are the problem" with political violence,

[^cite_note-NYTimes_20250912-188]

&#x20;cumulatively over decades, most extremist killings in the US have been caused by right-wing perpetrators.

[^cite_note-189]

&#x20;From 2022 through 2024, all 61 political killings were committed by right-wing extremists.

[^cite_note-AP_20250914-190]



Over decades, right wing ideologically motivated homicides have substantially outnumbered those perpetrated by left wing perpetrators in the US.

[^cite_note-CCJLS_20210726-191]

&#x20;Also, far-right motivated homicides have occurred much more frequently than [jihadi](7826589.md) violence inspired by [Islamic extremism](8734632.md) (not shown in chart).

[^cite_note-CCJLS_20210726-191]



[Ku Klux Klan](16779.md) parade in Washington, D.C., September 1926

Starting in the 1870s and continuing through the late 19th century, numerous [white supremacist](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/White_supremacism) [paramilitary groups](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Paramilitary_group) operated in [the South](179553.md), with the goal of organizing against and intimidating supporters of the [Republican Party](32070.md). Examples of such groups included the [Red Shirts](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Red_Shirts_\(Southern_United_States\)) and the [White League](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/White_League). The [Second Ku Klux Klan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Second_Ku_Klux_Klan), which was formed in 1915, combined [Protestant fundamentalism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Protestant_fundamentalism) and moralism with right-wing extremism. Its major support came from the urban South, [the Midwest](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Midwestern_United_States), and the [Pacific Coast](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/West_Coast_of_the_United_States).[^cite_note-FOOTNOTELipsetRaab1973116-192] While the Klan initially drew [upper middle class](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Upper_middle_class_in_the_United_States) support, its bigotry and violence alienated these members and it came to be dominated by less educated and poorer members.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTELipsetRaab1973125-193]
