Between the 1920s and the 1930s, the Ku Klux Klan developed an explicitly [nativist](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nativism_\(politics\)#United_States), pro-[Anglo-Saxon Protestant](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/White_Anglo-Saxon_Protestants), [anti-Catholic](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Anti-Catholicism), [anti-Irish](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Anti-Irish_sentiment), [anti-Italian](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Anti-Italianism), and [anti-Jewish](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Anti-Jewish_sentiment) stance in relation to the growing political, economic, and social uncertainty related to the [arrival of European immigrants on the American soil](15051.md), predominantly composed of [Irish people](775859.md), [Italians](385155.md), and [Eastern European Jews](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Immigration_of_Eastern_European_Jews).[^cite_note-194] The Ku Klux Klan claimed that there was a secret Catholic army within the United States loyal to the [Pope](23056.md), that one million [Knights of Columbus](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Knights_of_Columbus) were arming themselves, and that [Irish-American](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Irish-American) policemen would shoot [Protestants](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Protestantism_in_the_United_States) as heretics. Their sensationalistic claims eventually developed into full-blown [political conspiracy theories](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Conspiracy_theories_in_United_States_politics), to the point that the Klan claimed that [Roman Catholics were planning to take Washington and put the Vatican in power](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Anti-Catholicism_in_the_United_States) and that [all presidential assassinations](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_United_States_presidential_assassination_attempts_and_plots) had been carried out by Roman Catholics.[^cite_note-195][^cite_note-196]

The prominent Klan leader [D. C. Stephenson](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/D._C._Stephenson) believed in the [antisemitic canard](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Antisemitic_canard) of [Jewish control of finance](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jewish_control_of_finance), claiming that international Jewish bankers were behind the [World War I](4764461.md) and planned to destroy economic opportunities for Christians. Other Klansmen believed in the [Jewish Bolshevism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jewish_Bolshevism) conspiracy theory and claimed that the [Russian Revolution](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Russian_Revolution) and [communism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Communism) were orchestrated by [Jews](25955086.md). They frequently reprinted parts of *[The Protocols of the Elders of Zion](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/The_Protocols_of_the_Elders_of_Zion)* and [New York City](645042.md) was condemned as an evil city controlled by Jews and Roman Catholics. The objects of the Klan fear tended to vary by locale and included [African Americans](2154.md) as well as American [Roman Catholics](410868.md), [Jews](1131183.md), [labour unions](2474406.md), [liquor](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Temperance_movement), [Orientals](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Asian_Americans), and [Wobblies](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Industrial_Workers_of_the_World). They were also anti-elitist and attacked "the intellectuals", seeing themselves as egalitarian defenders of the common man.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTELipsetRaab1973138–139-197]

During the [Great Depression](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Great_Depression), there were a large number of small nativist groups, whose ideologies and bases of support were similar to those of earlier nativist groups. However, proto-fascist movements such as [Huey Long](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Huey_Long)'s [Share Our Wealth](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Share_Our_Wealth) and [Charles Coughlin](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Charles_Coughlin)'s [National Union for Social Justice](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/National_Union_for_Social_Justice_\(organization\)) emerged which differed from other right-wing groups by attacking big business, calling for economic reforms, and rejecting nativism. Coughlin's group later developed a [racist](2162835.md) ideology.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTELipsetRaab1973152-198]

During the [Cold War](325329.md) and the [Red Scares](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Red_Scare), the far right "saw spies and communists influencing government and entertainment. Thus, despite bipartisan [anticommunism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Anticommunism) in the United States, it was the right that mainly fought the great ideological battle against the communists."[^cite_note-FOOTNOTECarlisle2005588-199] The [John Birch Society](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/John_Birch_Society), founded in 1958, is a prominent example of a far-right organization mainly concerned with anti-communism and the perceived threat of communism. Neo-Nazi militant [Robert Jay Matthews](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Robert_Jay_Matthews) of the White supremacist group [The Order](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/The_Order_\(white_supremacist_group\)) came to support the John Birch Society, especially when [conservative](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Conservatism_in_the_United_States) icon [Barry Goldwater](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Barry_Goldwater) from Arizona ran for the presidency on the [Republican Party](32070.md) ticket. Far-right conservatives consider [John Birch](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/John_Birch_\(missionary\)) to be the first casualty of the Cold War.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTECarlisle2005557-200] In the 1990s, many conservatives turned against then-President [George H. W. Bush](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/George_H._W._Bush), who pleasured neither the Republican Party's more moderate and far-right wings. As a result, Bush was primared by [Pat Buchanan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pat_Buchanan). In the 2000s, critics of President [George W. Bush](3414021.md)'s conservative unilateralism argued it can be traced to both Vice President [Dick Cheney](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Dick_Cheney) who embraced the policy since the early 1990s and to far-right Congressmen who won their seats during the conservative revolution of 1994.[^cite_note-Kopeček_2007-19]

Although small voluntary militias had existed in the United States throughout the latter half of the 20th century, the groups became more popular during the early 1990s, after a series of standoffs between armed citizens and federal government agents, such as the 1992 [Ruby Ridge](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ruby_Ridge) siege and 1993 [Waco Siege](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Waco_Siege). These groups expressed concern for what they perceived as government tyranny within the United States and generally held [constitutionalist](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Constitutionalism_in_the_United_States), [libertarian](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Libertarianism_in_the_United_States), and [right-libertarian](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Right-libertarian) political views, with a strong focus on the Second Amendment gun rights and tax protest. They also embraced many of the same conspiracy theories as predecessor groups on the radical right, particularly the [New World Order](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/New_World_Order_\(conspiracy_theory\)) conspiracy theory. Examples of such groups are the patriot and militia movements [Oath Keepers](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Oath_Keepers) and the [Three Percenters](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Three_Percenters). A minority of militia groups, such as the [Aryan Nations](51669.md) and the [Posse Comitatus](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Posse_Comitatus_\(organization\)), were [White nationalists](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/White_nationalism) and saw militia and patriot movements as a form of White resistance against what they perceived to be a [liberal](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Modern_liberalism_in_the_United_States) and [multiculturalist](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Multiculturalist) government. Militia and patriot organizations were involved in the 2014 [Bundy standoff](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bundy_standoff)[^cite_note-201][^cite_note-202] and the 2016 [occupation of the Malheur National Wildlife Refuge](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Occupation_of_the_Malheur_National_Wildlife_Refuge).[^cite_note-203][^cite_note-204]



[National Socialist Movement](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/National_Socialist_Movement_\(United_States\)) rally on the west lawn of the [US Capitol](31979.md), Washington, DC, 2008

After the [September 11 attacks](5058690.md) in 2001, the [counter-jihad](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Counter-jihad) movement, supported by groups such as [Stop Islamization of America](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Stop_Islamization_of_America) and individuals such as [Frank Gaffney](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Frank_Gaffney) and [Pamela Geller](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pamela_Geller), began to gain traction among the American right. The counter-jihad members were widely dubbed "[Islamophobic](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Islamophobic)" for their [vocal criticism of the Islamic religion](2394308.md) and [its founder Muhammad](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Criticism_of_Muhammad),[^cite_note-Cimino_2005-205] and their belief that there was a significant threat posed by Muslims living in America.[^cite_note-Cimino_2005-205] Its proponents believed that the United States was under threat from "Islamic supremacism", accusing the [Council on American-Islamic Relations](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Council_on_American-Islamic_Relations) and even prominent conservatives such as [Suhail A. Khan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Suhail_A._Khan) and [Grover Norquist](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Grover_Norquist) of supporting [radical](8734632.md) [Islamist](15012.md) groups and organizations, such as the [Muslim Brotherhood](20742.md). The [alt-right](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Alt-right) emerged during the [2016 United States presidential election](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2016_United_States_presidential_election) cycle in support of the [Donald Trump](4848272.md)'s [presidential campaign](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2016_Donald_Trump_presidential_campaign) (*see*: [Trumpism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Trumpism)). It draws influence from [paleoconservatism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Paleoconservatism), [paleolibertarianism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Paleolibertarianism), [white nationalism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/White_nationalism), the [manosphere](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Manosphere), and the [Identitarian](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Identitarian_movement) and [neoreactionary](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Neoreactionary_movement) movements. The alt-right differs from previous radical right movements due to its heavy internet presence on websites such as [4chan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/4chan).[^cite_note-206]

Chetan Bhatt, in *White Extinction: Metaphysical Elements of Contemporary Western Fascism*, says that "The 'fear of white extinction', and related ideas of [population eugenics](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Eugenics), have travelled far and represent a wider political anxiety about 'white displacement' in the US, UK, and Europe that has fuelled the right-wing phenomena referred to by that sanitizing word '[populism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Populism)', a term that neatly evades attention to the racism and white majoritarianism that energizes it."[^cite_note-207]

### Asia

See also: [Fascism in Asia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fascism_in_Asia)

#### China

See also: [Chinese nationalism § Ultranationalism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Chinese_nationalism#Ultranationalism)

##### Republic of China (1912–1949)

[Dai Jitao Thought](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Dai_Jitao_Thought) is a collection of political ideas developed by the far-right politician [Dai Jitao](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Dai_Jitao);[^cite_note-208] this ideology is [Buddhist](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Buddhist_nationalism),[^cite_note-209] [conservative](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/National_conservatism),[^cite_note-210] and [Han Chinese nationalist](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Han_nationalism) based on [anti-communism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Anti-communism_in_China).[^cite_note-211]

##### People's Republic of China

In the 21st century, the [Chinese Communist Party](7175.md) (CCP) and [Chinese government](23239.md) has been criticized for promoting right-wing [authoritarian populist](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Authoritarian_populist)[^cite_note-212] or far-right[^cite_note-The_Diplomat-213] [Chinese nationalism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Chinese_nationalism) to justify oppression of the [Xinjiang](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Xinjiang), [Hong Kong](13404.md) region and a lack of [human rights](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Human_rights_in_China) improvement.[^cite_note-The_Diplomat-213] Under the leadership of [Xi Jinping](2017814.md), the Chinese Communist Party (CCP) has incorporated nationalist right-wing rhetoric into its state ideology, with some analysts noting that these narratives echo far-right populist themes found in European countries.[^cite_note-214]

Some scholars have argued that the similarities between the Chinese Communist Party in the 21st century and classical fascist regimes lie in their proximity to [state capitalism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/State_capitalism) (rather than orthodox [communism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Communism)), as well as their [anti-democratic](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Anti-democratic), [anti-labor](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Anti-labor), and [chauvinistic expansionism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Expansionist_nationalism),[^cite_note-215] but others have criticized the fascism label as "ahistorical" due to the absence of [mass mobilization](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mass_mobilization), along with its [Marxist-Leninist](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Marxist-Leninist) ideological roots.[^cite_note-216][^cite_note-217]

##### Diaspora politics

The [Epoch Times](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Epoch_Times) is an international multi-language newspaper with radical [anti-communist tendencies](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Anti-communism_in_China), associated with [Falun Gong](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Falun_Gong), a [new religious movement](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/New_religious_movement) that was banned and persecuted by the Chinese Communist Party. Since a shift in the newspaper's approach in 2016, the newspaper received significant attention for its favorable coverage of the [Trump administration](52231341.md),[^cite_note-NBCNews-218][^cite_note-Brown_2018-219] the [American far-right](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/American_far-right),[^cite_note-220] the [German far-right](18526492.md),[^cite_note-Allen-Ebrahimian-221][^cite_note-Winterbauer_2016-222] and the [French far-right](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/History_of_far-right_movements_in_France).[^cite_note-TheNewRepublic-223][^cite_note-Rougerie_2021-224]

#### India

Main article: [Hindutva](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hindutva)

| \[icon] | This section **needs expansion**. You can help by [making an edit request](https://en.wikipedia.org/w/index.php?title=Talk%3AFar-right_politics\&preload=Template%3ASubmit+an+edit+request%2Fpreload\&action=edit\&section=new\&editintro=Template%3AEdit+fully-protected%2Feditintro\&preloadtitle=Protected+edit+request+on+3+October+2026\&preloadparams%5B%5D=edit+fully-protected\&preloadparams%5B%5D=Far-right+politics)[adding missing information](https://en.wikipedia.org/w/index.php?title=Far-right_politics\&action=edit\&section=). *(September 2025)* |
| --- | --- |

The [Bharatiya Janata Party](149330.md) in India has been claimed to combine economic nationalism with [religious nationalism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Religious_nationalism).[^cite_note-225] It espouses the [Hindu nationalist](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hindu_nationalism) ideology of [Hindutva](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hindutva),[^cite_note-The_Hindutva_Road-226] which had borrowed ideas from [European fascism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/European_fascism).[^cite_note-227]

#### Indonesia

Some [Islamists](15012.md) in Indonesia are far-right.[^cite_note-k356-228]

#### Iran

The two main political camps in today's Iran are [Principlists](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Iranian_Principlists) and [Reformists](491098.md).[^cite_note-229] Principlists, especially "[Neoconservatives](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Iranian_Principlists#Factions)", have far-right and ultra-conservative views.[^cite_note-230]

**Iraq**

[Hawpa](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hawpa) is a Kurdish Neo-Nazi organization in Iraq.[^cite_note-231]

#### Israel

Main article: [Far-right politics in Israel](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Far-right_politics_in_Israel)

See also: [Neo-Zionism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Neo-Zionism)



Flag of Kach, used by Kahanists

[Kach](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kach_\(political_party\)) was a radical [Orthodox Jewish](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Orthodox_Judaism), [religious Zionist](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Religious_Zionist) [political party in Israel](240166.md), existing from 1971 to 1994.[^cite_note-232] Founded by Rabbi [Meir Kahane](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Meir_Kahane) in 1971, based on his Jewish-Orthodox-nationalist ideology subsequently known as [Kahanism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kahanism), which held the view that most [Arabs](2185.md) living in Israel are enemies of Jews and Israel itself, and believed that a [Jewish theocratic state](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Halakhic_state), where non-Jews have no voting rights, should be created.[^cite_note-233] The party secured a single seat in the [Knesset](74656.md) in the [1984 election](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1984_Israeli_legislative_election),[^cite_note-234] but was subsequently barred from standing in elections, and both it and Kahanist organizations were banned outright in 1994 by the [Israeli cabinet](5006115.md) under 1948 anti-[terrorism](30636.md) laws,[^cite_note-235] following statements by it in support of the 1994 [Cave of the Patriarchs massacre](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cave_of_the_Patriarchs_massacre) by a Kach supporter.[^cite_note-236]

In 2015, the Kach party and Kahanist movement were believed to have an overlapping membership of fewer than 100 people,[^cite_note-237][^cite_note-238] with links to the modern party [Otzma Yehudit](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Otzma_Yehudit) (Jewish Power) party,[^cite_note-239][^cite_note-240] which, running on a [Kahanist](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kahanist) and [anti-Arab](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Anti-Arab) platform,[^cite_note-241][^cite_note-242] won six seats in the [2022 Israeli legislative election](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2022_Israeli_legislative_election), having run jointly with fellow far-right parties [Religious Zionist Party](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Religious_Zionist_Party) and [Noam](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Noam_\(political_party\)).[^cite_note-243][^cite_note-244] The [thirty-seventh government of Israel](72545811.md) which formed after the 2022 Israeli legislative election as subsequently been critiqued as Israel's most hardline and far-right government to date.[^cite_note-245][^cite_note-246] The coalition government consists of six parties: [Likud](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Likud), [United Torah Judaism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/United_Torah_Judaism), [Shas](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Shas), Otzma Yehudit, Religious Zionist Party and Noam, so having half of its coalition partners hailing from the far-right. The government has been noted for its significant shift towards far-right policies, and the appointment of controversial far-right politicians, including [Itamar Ben-Gvir](54504115.md) and [Bezalel Smotrich](46196834.md), to positions of considerable influence.[^cite_note-247]

##### Ties with European far-right

There is also a complex relationship between Israel and the European far-right[^cite_note-248] that has been developing for more than 15 years.[^cite_note-249] The first major public sign of their alliance was in 2010 at an international far-right conference in Tel Aviv organized by a Likud party member.[^cite_note-250] A primary motivation is a shared anti-Islam ideology but there is also a common dislike of the European Union, of Arab and Muslim immigrants as well as support for undermining democracy and installing autocratic, or worse, rulers and regimes. "Yair Netanyahu, the prime minister's son, last week called for the death of the European Union and the return of a 'Christian' Europe."[^cite_note-251] Other details suggest a deeper collaboration between the [Likud party](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Likud) and the [German AfD](38708229.md). "In 2019, the Bundestag passed a resolution condemning B.D.S. as antisemitic .The history of the resolution is telling. A version was originally introduced by the AfD".[^cite_note-252] Netanyahu's government has actively cultivated relations with various European far-right parties and leaders, including Vlaams Belang, Attack, the Freedom Party of Austria, the Alliance for the Union of Romanians and the Sweden Democrats.[^cite_note-253][^cite_note-254]

These parties offer strong support for Israel's hardline policies towards Palestinians, its opposition to Palestinian statehood, and its pro-settlement stance.[^cite_note-255][^cite_note-256] Netanyahu has also cultivated a particularly strong bond with [Viktor Orbán](750582.md)'s [Fidesz](404647.md) party in Hungary, a key figure in the European far-right landscape.[^cite_note-257] The [Likud party](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Likud) recently joined the [Patriots for Europe](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Patriots_for_Europe) alliance in the [European Parliament](9581.md) as an observer member.[^cite_note-258]

#### Japan

Main articles: [Uyoku dantai](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Uyoku_dantai) and [Ultranationalism (Japan)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ultranationalism_\(Japan\))

See also: [Japanese nationalism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Japanese_nationalism) and [Kokkashugi](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kokkashugi)



Gaisen Uyoku (街宣右翼), a Japanese far-right group, holding an [anti-China speech](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Anti-Chinese_sentiment_in_Japan) at the square of [Kinshichō Station](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kinshich%C5%8D_Station) in [Sumida, Tokyo](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sumida,_Tokyo) (2010)

In 1996, the [National Police Agency](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/National_Police_Agency_\(Japan\)) estimated that there were over 1,000 extremist right-wing groups in [Japan](15573.md), with about 100,000 members in total. These groups are known in Japanese as *Uyoku dantai*. While there are political differences among the groups, they generally carry a philosophy of [anti-leftism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Anti-leftism), [hostility towards China](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Anti-Chinese_sentiment_in_Japan), [North Korea and South Korea](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Anti-Korean_sentiment_in_Japan), and justification of [Japan's role](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Japan_during_World_War_II) and [war crimes](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Japanese_war_crimes) in World War II. *Uyoku dantai* groups are well known for their highly visible [propaganda vehicles fitted with loudspeakers](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sound_trucks_in_Japan) and prominently marked with the name of the group and propaganda slogans. The vehicles play patriotic or wartime-era Japanese songs. Activists affiliated with such groups have used [Molotov cocktails](20922.md) and [time bombs](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Time_bomb) to intimidate moderate Japanese politicians and public figures, including former Deputy Foreign Minister Hitoshi Tanaka and [Fuji Xerox](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fuji_Xerox) Chairman [Yotaro Kobayashi](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Yotaro_Kobayashi). An ex-member of a right-wing group set fire to [Liberal Democratic Party](164231.md) politician [Koichi Kato](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Koichi_Kato_\(politician,_born_1939\))'s house. Koichi Kato and Yotaro Kobayashi had spoken out against Koizumi's visits to [Yasukuni Shrine](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Yasukuni_Shrine).[^cite_note-259] Openly revisionist, [Nippon Kaigi](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nippon_Kaigi) is considered "the biggest right-wing organization in Japan".[^cite_note-260][^cite_note-261]

#### Malaysia

Far-right [non-governmental organizations](46539.md) have been appropriating [human rights](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Human_right) language in Malaysia.[^cite_note-c820-262]

#### South Korea

See also: [Ilminism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ilminism), [Radical anti-communism § In South Korean politics](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Radical_anti-communism#In_South_Korean_politics), and [South Korean nationalism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/South_Korean_nationalism)

Since the founding of the [South Korea](27019.md) in 1948, [authoritarian conservative](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Authoritarian_conservative) dictatorships such as [Syngman Rhee](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Syngman_Rhee), [Park Chung-hee](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Park_Chung-hee), and [Chun Doo-hwan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Chun_Doo-hwan) have continued until 1987.

[Yoon Suk Yeol](62877952.md), who served as South Korea's president from 2022 until his removal from office in 2025, faced significant criticism from opponents and commentators for embodying far-right political rhetoric and views.[^cite_note-263][^cite_note-far-right_tendencies-264][^cite_note-265] In 2024, a [South Korean martial law crisis](78508419.md) occurred under the then President Yoon when he accused the liberal [Democratic Party](42316753.md), which held a majority in the [National Assembly](601423.md), of engaging in "anti-state activities" and collaborating with "North Korean communists" to undermine the constitutional order.[^cite_note-266]

In the 20th century, [right-wing authoritarian regimes](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Right-wing_dictatorship) such as those of Rhee and Park, combined "pure-blood" ethno-nationalism with state-nationalism.[^cite_note-Park-267] While 21st-century South Korean far-right groups retain strong [anti-North Korean sentiments](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Anti%E2%80%93North_Korean_sentiment#South_Korea), they increasingly prioritize South Korea-centered "[state nationalism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/South_Korean_nationalism)" (국가주의; 國家主義) over traditional "[Korean ethnic nationalism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Korean_ethnic_nationalism)" (민족주의; 民族主義);[^cite_note-272][^cite_note-273][^cite_note-274] however, at the same time, it often exhibits [racist or ethnic exclusion attitudes](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Anti-Chinese_sentiment_in_Korea) toward [Chinese people](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Chinese_people_in_Korea).[^cite_note-275] In South Korean conservative political thought, this emphasis on "state nationalism" has been analyzed by scholars as having been partially influenced by the Japanese pre-1945 concept of *[kokkashugi](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kokkashugi)* (国家主義).[^cite_note-276][^cite_note-277][^cite_note-278]

#### Taiwan (Republic of China)

##### Before 1992

See also: [Chiangism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Chiangism) and [White Terror (Taiwan)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/White_Terror_\(Taiwan\))

In 1947, the [February 28 incident](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/February_28_incident) was created by the [Kuomintang](16903.md)–led [nationalist government](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nationalist_government). In the aftermath of this incident, [martial law](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Martial_law_in_Taiwan) was enforced in Taiwan from 1949, and the [Great Retreat](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Retreat_of_the_government_of_Republic_of_China_to_Taiwan) took place the same year. [Chiang Kai-shek](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Chiang_Kai-shek) ruled authoritarian conservative, [anti-communist](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Anti-communism_in_China#Taiwan) and [Chinese ultranationalist](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Chinese_ultranationalist). Until martial law was lifted in 1987, [Taiwanese nationalists](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Taiwanese_nationalist), [leftists](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Leftist) and [liberals](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Liberalism) were politically suppressed.

##### After 1992

See also: [Pro-Beijing camp (Taiwan)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pro-Beijing_camp_\(Taiwan\)) and [United front in Taiwan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/United_front_in_Taiwan)



Party flag of [Patriot Alliance Association](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Patriot_Alliance_Association) (PAA)

In modern Taiwanese politics after [1992 Consensus](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1992_Consensus), the 'mainstream' political left advocated [Taiwanese nationalism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Taiwanese_nationalism) (including [independence](31156.md)) and the political right defended [Chinese nationalism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Chinese_nationalism) (including [unification](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Chinese_unification)).[^cite_note-279] As a result, Taiwan's political landscape is somewhat unique from Western countries; the "far-right" [New Party](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/New_Party_\(Taiwan\)),[^cite_note-280] [Patriot Alliance Association](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Patriot_Alliance_Association),[^cite_note-蕭嘉弘-281] [Chinese Unification Promotion Party](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Chinese_Unification_Promotion_Party),[^cite_note-蕭嘉弘-281][^cite_note-282] and others advocate for [one country, two systems](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/One_country,_two_systems), the unification policy proposed by the [Chinese Communist Party](7175.md).[^cite_note-283][^cite_note-284] These far-right \[Chinese] nationalists are sometimes referred to as "radical pro-unification factions" ([Chinese](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Traditional_Chinese_characters): 急統派).[^cite_note-蕭嘉弘-281] By contrast, most politicians in the [centre-right](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Centre-right) conservative [Kuomintang](16903.md) after the [Taiwanese democratization](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/History_of_Taiwan#Democratic_reforms) reject one country, two systems.[^cite_note-285][^cite_note-286]

Some radical Taiwanese nationalists are also considered far-right: the [Taiwan Statebuilding Party](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Taiwan_Statebuilding_Party) is officially a "left-wing" in support of Taiwanese independence, but is also referred to as "far-right" due to [anti-Chinese nativism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nativism_\(politics\)#Taiwan);[^cite_note-287][^cite_note-288][^cite_note-289] the [Taiwanese Localism Front](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Taiwanese_Localism_Front), a radical anti-communist organization, is also referred to as the far-right;[^cite_note-工商時報-290][^cite_note-未命名-20230318191509-291] ultra-nationalistic actions of the pro-independence [Pan-Green Coalition](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pan-Green_Coalition) (led by the [Democratic Progressive Party](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Democratic_Progressive_Party)) have been dubbed the "[Green Terror](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Green_Terror_\(Taiwan\))".[^cite_note-292]

### Europe

See also: [Fascism in Europe](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fascism_in_Europe)

#### Armenia

The [Armenian-Aryan Racialist Political Movement](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hosank) and the [Adequate Party](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Adequate_Party) are the main far-right political movements in [Armenia](10918072.md).[^cite_note-293][^cite_note-294]

#### Croatia

Main article: [Far-right politics in Croatia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Far-right_politics_in_Croatia)

Individuals and groups in Croatia that employ far-right politics are most often associated with the historical [Ustaše](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Usta%C5%A1e) movement, hence they have connections to [neo-Nazism](54361.md) and [neo-fascism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Neo-fascism). That World War II political movement was an extremist organization at the time supported by the [German Nazis](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nazi_Party) and the [Italian Fascists](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/National_Fascist_Party). The association with the Ustaše has been called neo-Ustashism by [Slavko Goldstein](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Slavko_Goldstein).[^cite_note-295] Most active far-right political parties in Croatia openly state their continuity with the Ustaše.[^cite_note-Eremina_&_Seredenko-296] These include the [Croatian Party of Rights](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Croatian_Party_of_Rights) and [Authentic Croatian Party of Rights](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Authentic_Croatian_Party_of_Rights).[^cite_note-Eremina_&_Seredenko-296] Croatia's far-right often advocates the false theory that the [Jasenovac concentration camp](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jasenovac_concentration_camp) was a "labour camp" where mass murder did not take place.[^cite_note-Milekic-297]

The coalition led by [Miroslav Škoro](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Miroslav_%C5%A0koro)'s far-right [Homeland Movement](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Homeland_Movement_\(Croatia\)) came third at the [2020 parliamentary election](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2020_Croatian_parliamentary_election), winning 10.9% of the vote and 16 seats.[^cite_note-298][^cite_note-299]

#### Estonia



General [Andres Larka](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Andres_Larka) speaking in 1933

Estonia's most significant far-right movement was the [Vaps](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Vaps) movement. Its ideological predecessor Valve Liit was founded by Admiral [Johan Pitka](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Johan_Pitka) and later banned for maligning the government. The organization became politicized quickly Vaps soon turned into a mass fascist movement.[^cite_note-300] In 1933, Estonians voted on Vaps' proposed changes to the constitution and the party later won a large proportion of the vote. However, the State Elder Konstantin Päts declared state of emergency and imprisoned the leadership of the Vaps. In 1935, all political parties were banned. In 1935, a Vaps coup attempt was discovered, which led to the banning of the Finnish [Patriotic People's Movement](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Patriotic_People's_Movement)'s [youth wing](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Blues-and-Blacks) that had been secretly aiding and arming them.[^cite_note-301][^cite_note-302]



Far-right torch march in Tallinn

During World War II, the [Estonian Self-Administration](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Estonian_Self-Administration) was a collaborationist pro-Nazi government set up in Estonia, headed by Vaps member [Hjalmar Mäe](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hjalmar_M%C3%A4e).[^cite_note-303] In the 21st century, the coalition-governing [Conservative People's Party of Estonia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Conservative_People's_Party_of_Estonia) been described as far right.[^cite_note-304] The neo-Nazi terrorist organization [Feuerkrieg Division](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Feuerkrieg_Division) was found and operates in the country, with some members of the Conservative People's Party of Estonia having been linked to the Feuerkrieg Division.[^cite_note-305][^cite_note-306][^cite_note-307][^cite_note-308] The party's youth organization [Blue Awakening](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Blue_Awakening) organizes an annual torchlight march through [Tallinn](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tallinn) on Estonia's [Independence Day](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Independence_Day_\(Estonia\)). The event has been harshly criticized by the [Simon Wiesenthal Center](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Simon_Wiesenthal_Center) that described it as "Nuremberg-esque" and likened the ideology of the participants to that of the [Estonian Nazi collaborators](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Collaboration_with_Nazi_Germany_and_Fascist_Italy#Estonia).[^cite_note-309][^cite_note-310]

#### Finland

Main article: [Far-right politics in Finland](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Far-right_politics_in_Finland)



The [Peasant March](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Peasant_March), a show of force in Helsinki by the [Lapua Movement](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lapua_Movement) on 7 July 1930

In Finland, support for the far right was most widespread between 1920 and 1940 when the [Academic Karelia Society](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Academic_Karelia_Society), [Lapua Movement](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lapua_Movement), [Patriotic People's Movement](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Patriotic_People's_Movement) and [Vientirauha](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Vientirauha) operated in the country and had hundreds of thousands of members.[^cite_note-311] Far-right groups exercised considerable political power during this period, pressuring the government to outlaw communist parties and newspapers and expel [Freemasons](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Freemasons) from the armed forces.[^cite_note-312][^cite_note-313] During the Cold War, all parties deemed fascist were banned according to the [Paris Peace Treaties](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Paris_Peace_Treaties) and all former fascist activists had to find new political homes.[^cite_note-314] Despite [Finlandization](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Finlandization), many continued in public life. Three former members of the Waffen SS served as ministers of defense; [Sulo Suorttanen](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sulo_Suorttanen) and [Pekka Malinen](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pekka_Malinen) as well as [Mikko Laaksonen](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mikko_Laaksonen).[^cite_note-315][^cite_note-316]



Captain [Arvi Kalsta](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Arvi_Kalsta) addressing an [SKJ](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Finnish_People's_Organisation) meeting

The skinhead culture gained momentum during the late 1980s and peaked during the late 1990s. Numerous hate crimes were committed against refugees, including a number of racially motivated murders.[^cite_note-317][^cite_note-318]

Today, the most prominent neo-Nazi group is the [Nordic Resistance Movement](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nordic_Resistance_Movement), which is tied to multiple murders, attempted murders and assaults of political enemies was found in 2006 and proscribed in 2019. Prominent far-right parties include the [Blue-and-Black Movement](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Blue-and-Black_Movement) and [Power Belongs to the People](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Power_Belongs_to_the_People).[^cite_note-319] The second biggest Finnish party, the [Finns Party](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Finns_Party), has been described as far right.[^cite_note-finns-320][^cite_note-321][^cite_note-322][^cite_note-323] The former leader of the Finns party and current speaker of the Parliament [Jussi Halla-aho](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jussi_Halla-aho), has been convicted of hate speech due to his comments stating that, "Prophet Muhammad was a pedophile and Islam justifies pedophilia and Pedophilia was Allah's will." Finns Party members have frequently supported far-right and neo-Nazi movements such as the Finnish Defense League, Soldiers of Odin, Nordic Resistance Movement, Rajat Kiinni (Close the Borders), and Suomi Ensin (Finland First)."[^cite_note-324] In the 1990s and 2000s, before the breakthrough of the Finns Party, a few neo-Nazi candidates enjoyed success, like Janne Kujala of [Finland - Fatherland](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Finland_-_Fatherland) (founded as Aryan Germanic Brotherhood) and [Jouni Lanamäki](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jouni_Lanam%C3%A4ki) who was previously associated with the [Nordic Reich Party](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nordic_Reich_Party).[^cite_note-hs2017-325][^cite_note-326] [Pekka Siitoin](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pekka_Siitoin) of the [National Democracy Party](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/National_Democratic_Party_\(Finland\)) was the fifth most popular candidate in [Naantali](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Naantali) city council elections.[^cite_note-327]

The NRM and Finns party and other far-right groups organize [an annual torch march demonstration](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/612_march) in Helsinki in memory of the Finnish SS-battalion on the [Finnish independence day](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Independence_Day_\(Finland\)) which ends at the [Hietaniemi cemetery](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hietaniemi_cemetery) where members visit the tomb of [Carl Gustaf Emil Mannerheim](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Carl_Gustaf_Emil_Mannerheim) and the monument to the [Finnish SS Battalion](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Finnish_SS_Battalion).[^cite_note-328][^cite_note-329] The event is protested by antifascists, leading to counterdemonstrators being violently assaulted by NRM members who act as security. The demonstration attracts close to 3,000 participants according to the estimates of the police and hundreds of officers patrol Helsinki to prevent violent clashes.[^cite_note-330][^cite_note-331][^cite_note-holappa-332][^cite_note-333]

#### France

Main article: [Far-right politics in France](9701975.md)



A [Génération Identitaire](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Les_Identitaires#Youth_wing) demonstration in France, 2017

The largest far-right party in Europe is the French anti-immigration party [National Rally](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/National_Rally_\(France\)), formally known as the National Front.[^cite_note-334][^cite_note-335] The party was founded in 1972, uniting a variety of French far-right groups under the leadership of [Jean-Marie Le Pen](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jean-Marie_Le_Pen).[^cite_note-336] Since 1984, it has been the major force of [French nationalism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/French_nationalism).[^cite_note-FOOTNOTECamusLebourg2017\[\[Category\:Wikipedia_articles_needing_page_number_citations_from_August_2021\]\]<sup_class="noprint_Inline-Template_"_style="white-space\:nowrap;">\[<i>\[\[Wikipedia\:Citing_sources|<span_title="This_citation_requires_a_reference_to_the_specific_page_or_range_of_pages_in_which_the_material_appears. (August_2021)">page needed</span>\]\]</i>\]</sup>-337] Jean-Marie Le Pen's daughter [Marine Le Pen](572897.md) was elected to succeed him as party leader in 2012. Under Jean-Marie Le Pen's leadership, the party sparked outrage for hate speech, including [Holocaust denial](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Holocaust_denial) and [Islamophobia](162173.md).[^cite_note-338][^cite_note-339]

#### Germany

Main article: [Far-right politics in Germany (1945–present)](18526492.md)



Right-wing populists protesting against Muslims in Germany, 2008

In 1945, the [Allied powers](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Allies_of_World_War_II) took control of Germany and banned the [swastika](45943.md), [Nazi Party](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nazi_Party) and the publication of *[Mein Kampf](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mein_Kampf)*. Explicitly [Nazi](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nazi) and [neo-Nazi](54361.md) organizations are banned in Germany.[^cite_note-naziban-340] In 1960, the West German parliament voted unanimously to "make it illegal to incite hatred, to provoke violence, or to insult, ridicule or defame 'parts of the population' in a manner apt to breach the peace". German law outlaws anything that "approves of, glorifies or justifies the violent and despotic rule of the National Socialists".[^cite_note-naziban-340] Section 86a of the *[Strafgesetzbuch](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Strafgesetzbuch)* (Criminal Code) outlaws any "use of symbols of unconstitutional organizations" outside the contexts of "art or science, research or teaching". The law primarily outlaws the use of Nazi symbols, flags, insignia, uniforms, slogans and forms of greeting.[^cite_note-iuscomp-341] In the 21st century, the German far right consists of various small parties and two larger groups, namely [Alternative for Germany](38708229.md) (AfD)[^cite_note-farright-342] [Pegida](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pegida).[^cite_note-naziban-340][^cite_note-Virchow-343][^cite_note-344][^cite_note-345]

In March 2021, the Germany domestic intelligence agency [Federal Office for the Protection of the Constitution](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Federal_Office_for_the_Protection_of_the_Constitution) placed the AfD under surveillance, the first time in the post-war period that a main opposition party had been subjected to such scrutiny.[^cite_note-346]

In contemporary Germany, Far-right parties such as [National Democratic Party of Germany](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/National_Democratic_Party_of_Germany) (NPD), [German People's Union](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/German_People's_Union) (DVU) and [Alternative for Germany](38708229.md) (AfD) are stronger in [eastern Germany](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/New_states_of_Germany).

#### Greece

##### Metaxism

Main article: [Metaxism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Metaxism)



[Ioannis Metaxas](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ioannis_Metaxas)

The far right in Greece first came to power under the ideology of Metaxism, a [proto-fascist](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Proto-fascist) ideology developed by dictator [Ioannis Metaxas](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ioannis_Metaxas).[^cite_note-stanpayne-347] Metaxism called for the regeneration of the Greek nation and the establishment of an ethnically homogeneous state.[^cite_note-Sorensen-348] Metaxism disparaged [liberalism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Liberalism), and held individual interests to be subordinate to those of the nation, seeking to mobilize the Greek people as a disciplined mass in service to the creation of a "new Greece".[^cite_note-Sorensen-348]

The Metaxas government and its official doctrines are often compared to conventional totalitarian-conservative dictatorships such as [Francisco Franco](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Francisco_Franco)'s [Spain](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Francoist_Spain) or [António de Oliveira Salazar](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ant%C3%B3nio_de_Oliveira_Salazar)'s [Portugal](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Estado_Novo_\(Portugal\)).[^cite_note-stanpayne-347][^cite_note-European_Dictatorships,_1918–1945-349] The Metaxist government derived its authority from the conservative establishment and its doctrines strongly supported traditional institutions such as the [Greek Orthodox Church](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Greek_Orthodox_Church) and the [Greek Royal Family](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Greek_Royal_Family); essentially [reactionary](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Reactionary), it lacked the radical theoretical dimensions of ideologies such as [Italian Fascism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Italian_Fascism) and [German Nazism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nazism).[^cite_note-stanpayne-347][^cite_note-European_Dictatorships,_1918–1945-349]

##### Axis occupation of Greece and aftermath

Main article: [Axis occupation of Greece](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Axis_occupation_of_Greece)



German soldiers in 1941 raising the [German War Flag](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Reichskriegsflagge) over the [Acropolis](2076.md) which would be taken down by [Manolis Glezos](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Manolis_Glezos) and [Apostolos Santas](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Apostolos_Santas) in one of the first acts of resistance

The Metaxis regime came to an end after the Axis powers invaded Greece. The Axis occupation of Greece began in April 1941.[^cite_note-350] The occupation ruined the Greek economy and brought about terrible hardships for the Greek civilian population.[^cite_note-351] The Jewish population of Greece was nearly eradicated. Of its pre-war population of 75–77,000, only around 11–12,000 survived, either by joining the resistance or being hidden.[^cite_note-352] Following the short-lived interim government of [Georgios Papandreou](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Georgios_Papandreou), the military seized power in Greece during the [1967 Greek coup d'état](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1967_Greek_coup_d'%C3%A9tat), replacing the interim government with the right-wing United States-backed [Greek junta](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Greek_junta). The Junta was a series of [military juntas](216815.md) that ruled Greece from 1967 to 1974. The [dictatorship](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Dictatorship) was characterized by right-wing cultural policies, restrictions on [civil liberties](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Civil_liberties) and the imprisonment, torture and exile of [political opponents](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Greek_anti-junta_movement). The junta's rule ended on 24 July 1974 under the pressure of the [Turkish invasion of Cyprus](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Turkish_invasion_of_Cyprus), leading to the [Metapolitefsi](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Metapolitefsi) ("regime change") to democracy and the establishment of the [Third Hellenic Republic](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Third_Hellenic_Republic).[^cite_note-353][^cite_note-354]

Until 2019, the dominant far-right party in Greece in the 21st century was the [neo-Nazi](54361.md)[^cite_note-355][^cite_note-356][^cite_note-357][^cite_note-358][^cite_note-359][^cite_note-360][^cite_note-361] and Mataxist inspired[^cite_note-362][^cite_note-363][^cite_note-364][^cite_note-365][^cite_note-366] [Golden Dawn](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Golden_Dawn_\(political_party\)).[^cite_note-367][^cite_note-368][^cite_note-369][^cite_note-370][^cite_note-371] At the [May 2012 Greek legislative election](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/May_2012_Greek_legislative_election), Golden Dawn won 21 seats in the Hellenic Parliament, receiving 6.97% of the vote.[^cite_note-372][^cite_note-373] It became the third largest party in the Greek Parliament with 17 seats after the [January 2015 election](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/January_2015_Greek_legislative_election), winning 6.28% of the vote.[^cite_note-374]

Founded by [Nikolaos Michaloliakos](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nikolaos_Michaloliakos), Golden Dawn had its origins in the movement that worked towards a return to right-wing military dictatorship in Greece. Following an investigation into the 2013 [murder of Pavlos Fyssas](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Murder_of_Pavlos_Fyssas), an anti-fascist rapper, by a supporter of the party,[^cite_note-375] Michaloliakos and several other Golden Dawn parliamentarians and members were arrested and held in [pre-trial detention](14632360.md) on suspicion of forming a criminal organization.[^cite_note-376] The trial began on 20 April 2015[^cite_note-377] and eventually led to the conviction of 7 of its leaders for heading a criminal organization and 61 other defendants for participating in a criminal organization.[^cite_note-378] Guilty verdicts on charges of murder, attempted murder, and violent attacks on immigrants and left-wing political opponents were also delivered and prison sentences of a combined total of over 500 years were handed out.

Golden Dawn later lost all of its remaining seats in the Greek Parliament in the [2019 Greek legislative election](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2019_Greek_legislative_election), and[^cite_note-379] a 2020 survey showed the party's popularity plummeting to just 1.5%, down from 2.9% in previous year's elections.[^cite_note-380] This means that the largest party in Greece that is considered right wing to far right is [Greek Solution](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Greek_Solution), which has been described as ideologically [ultranationalist](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ultranationalist)[^cite_note-PoliticoUnorthodox-381][^cite_note-382] and [right-wing populist](8354382.md).[^cite_note-383] The party garnered 3.7% of the vote in the 2019 Greek legislative election, winning 10 out of the 300 seats in the [Hellenic Parliament](2449826.md) and 4.18% of the vote in the [2019 European Parliament election in Greece](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2019_European_Parliament_election_in_Greece), winning one seat in the [European Parliament](9581.md).[^cite_note-384]

#### Italy

Main article: [Armed, far-right organizations in Italy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Armed,_far-right_organizations_in_Italy)

The far right has maintained a continuous political presence in Italy since the fall of Mussolini. The [neo-fascist](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Neo-fascist) party [Italian Social Movement](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Italian_Social_Movement) (1946–1995), influenced by the previous [Italian Social Republic](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Italian_Social_Republic) (1943–1945), became one of the chief reference points for the European far-right from the end of World War II until the late 1980s.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEIgnazi200351-385]

[Silvio Berlusconi](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Silvio_Berlusconi) and his [Forza Italia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Forza_Italia_\(1994\)) party dominated politics from 1994. According to some scholars, it gave neo-fascism a new respectability. Caio Giulio Cesare Mussolini, great-grandson of [Benito Mussolini](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Benito_Mussolini), stood for the [2019 European Parliament election](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2019_European_Parliament_election_in_Italy) as a member of the far right [Brothers of Italy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Brothers_of_Italy) party.[^cite_note-BBCMuss-386] In 2011, it was estimated that the neo-fascist [CasaPound](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/CasaPound) party had 5,000 members.[^cite_note-387] The name is derived from the fascist poet [Ezra Pound](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ezra_Pound). It has also been influenced by the [Manifesto of Verona](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Congress_of_Verona_\(1943\)), the [Labour Charter of 1927](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Labour_Charter_of_1927) and social legislation of fascism.[^cite_note-388] There has been collaboration between CasaPound and the [identitarian movement](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Identitarian_movement).[^cite_note-389]

The [European migrant crisis](46415102.md) has become an increasingly divisive issue in Italy.[^cite_note-390] Interior Minister [Matteo Salvini](1767165.md) has been courting far-right voters. His [Northern League](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Northern_League_\(Italy\)) party has become an [anti-immigrant](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Anti-immigrant), [nationalist](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nationalist) movement. Both parties are using Mussolini nostalgia to further their aims.[^cite_note-BBCMuss-386]

#### Netherlands

Main articles: [Netherlands in World War II](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Netherlands_in_World_War_II) and [Fascism in the Netherlands](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fascism_in_the_Netherlands)

Despite being neutral, the Netherlands was invaded by [Nazi Germany](21212.md) on 10 May 1940 as part of [Fall Gelb](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fall_Gelb).[^cite_note-391] About 70% of the country's Jewish population were killed during the occupation, a much higher percentage than comparable countries such as Belgium and France.[^cite_note-392] Most of the south of the country was liberated in the second half of 1944. The rest, especially the west and north of the country still under occupation, suffered from a famine at the end of 1944 known as the [Hunger Winter](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Dutch_famine_of_1944). On 5 May 1945, the whole country was finally liberated by the [total surrender of all German forces](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/German_Instrument_of_Surrender). Since the end of World War II, the Netherlands has had a number of small far-right groups and parties, the largest and most successful being the [Party for Freedom](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Party_for_Freedom) led by [Geert Wilders](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Geert_Wilders).[^cite_note-393] Other far-right Dutch groups include the neo-Nazi [Dutch People's Union](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Dutch_People's_Union) (1973–present),[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEMudde2002118–122-394] the [Centre Party](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Centre_Party_\(Netherlands\)) (1982–1986), the [Centre Party '86](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Centre_Party_'86) (1986–1998), the [Dutch Block](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Dutch_Block) (1992–2000), [New National Party](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/New_National_Party_\(Netherlands\)) (1998–2005), the ultranationalist [National Alliance](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/National_Alliance_\(Netherlands\)) (2003–2007).[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEMudde2002\[\[Category\:Wikipedia_articles_needing_page_number_citations_from_August_2021\]\]<sup_class="noprint_Inline-Template_"_style="white-space\:nowrap;">\[<i>\[\[Wikipedia\:Citing_sources|<span_title="This_citation_requires_a_reference_to_the_specific_page_or_range_of_pages_in_which_the_material_appears. (August_2021)">page needed</span>\]\]</i>\]</sup>-395][^cite_note-396] and various overlapping Defend groups (2021-present)[^cite_note-397]

#### Poland

Main article: [Far-right politics in Poland](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Far-right_politics_in_Poland)



[National Radical Camp](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/National_Radical_Camp_\(1993\)) march in Kraków, July 2007

Following the collapse of [Communist Poland](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Communist_Poland), a number of far-right groups came to prominence including The [National Revival of Poland](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/National_Revival_of_Poland), the [European National Front](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/European_National_Front), the [Association for Tradition and Culture "Niklot"](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Association_for_Tradition_and_Culture_%22Niklot%22).[^cite_note-398] The [All-Polish Youth](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/All-Polish_Youth) and [National Radical Camp](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/National_Radical_Camp_\(1993\)) were recreated in 1989 and 1993, respectively becoming Poland's most prominent far-right organizations. In 1995, the [Anti-Defamation League](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Anti-Defamation_League) estimated the number of far-right and [white power](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/White_power) skinheads in Poland at 2,000.[^cite_note-399] Since late 2000s smaller fascist groups have merged to form the neo-Nazi [Autonome Nationalisten](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Autonome_Nationalisten). A number of far-right parties have run candidates in elections including the [League of Polish Families](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/League_of_Polish_Families), the [National Movement](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/National_Movement_\(Poland\)) with limited success.[^cite_note-400]

In 2019, the [Confederation Liberty and Independence](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Confederation_Liberty_and_Independence) earned 1,256,953 votes which was 6.81% of the total vote in an election that saw a historically high turnout. Members of far-right groups make up a significant portion of those taking part in the annual Independence March in central Warsaw which started in 2009 to mark [Independence Day](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Independence_Day_\(Poland\)). About 60,000 were in the 2017 march marking the 99th anniversary of independence, with placards such as "Clean Blood" seen on the march.[^cite_note-wp-20171113-401] [Law and Justice](520309.md), the previous governing party of Poland, has sometimes been described as far-right,[^cite_note-404] although it is also considered centre-right instead,[^cite_note-405] or it is argued that the party is not far-right,[^cite_note-406] with political scientist Michael Minkerberg arguing that the party is "not a radical right party but right-wing populist".[^cite_note-407]

#### Romania

Main article: [Greater Romania Party](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Greater_Romania_Party)

The preeminent far-right party in Romania is the Greater Romania Party, founded in 1991 by Tudor, who was formerly known as a "[court poet](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Poet_laureate)" of [Communist](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Romanian_Communist_Party) dictator [Nicolae Ceaușescu](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nicolae_Ceau%C8%99escu)[^cite_note-408] and his literary mentor, the writer [Eugen Barbu](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Eugen_Barbu), one year after Tudor launched the *România Mare* weekly magazine, which remains the most important propaganda tool of the PRM. Tudor subsequently launched a companion daily newspaper called *Tricolorul*. The historical expression *[Greater Romania](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Greater_Romania)* refers to the idea of recreating the former [Kingdom of Romania](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kingdom_of_Romania) which existed during the interwar period. Having been the largest entity to bear the name of *Romania*, the frontiers were marked with the intent of uniting most territories inhabited by [ethnic Romanians](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Romanians) into a single country and it is now a rallying cry for [Romanian nationalists](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Romanian_nationalists). Due to internal conditions under [Communist Romania](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Communist_Romania) after World War II, the expression's use was forbidden in publications until after the [Romanian Revolution](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Romanian_Revolution) in 1989. The party's initial success was partly attributed to the deep rootedness of Ceaușescu's [national communism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/National_communism) in Romania.[^cite_note-409]

Both the ideology and the main political focus of the Greater Romania Party are reflected in frequently strongly nationalistic articles written by Tudor. The party has called for the outlawing of the ethnic Hungarian party, the [Democratic Union of Hungarians in Romania](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Democratic_Union_of_Hungarians_in_Romania), for allegedly plotting the secession of Transylvania.[^cite_note-410]

#### Russia

Main article: [Far-right politics in Russia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Far-right_politics_in_Russia)

The period of development of Russian fascism in the 1930s–1940s was characterized by sympathy for [Italian fascism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Italian_fascism) and German [Nazism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nazism) and pronounced anti-communism and [antisemitism](1078.md).



The [Russian Fascist Party](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Russian_Fascist_Party) in the first half of the 20th century. The slogan "Let's get our homeland!" is also used by the modern far-right in Russia.

Russian fascism has its roots in the movements known in history as the [Black Hundreds](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Black_Hundreds) and the [White movement](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/White_movement). It was distributed among [white émigré](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/White_%C3%A9migr%C3%A9) circles living in Germany, [Manchukuo](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Manchukuo), and the United States. In Germany and the United States (unlike Manchukuo), they practically did not conduct political activity, limiting themselves to the publication of newspapers and brochures.

Some ideologues of the white movement, such as [Ivan Ilyin](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ivan_Ilyin) and [Vasily Shulgin](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Vasily_Shulgin), welcomed the coming to power of Mussolini in Italy and Hitler in Germany, offering their comrades-in-arms the fascist "method" as a way to fight socialism, communism, and [godlessness](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Atheism). At the same time, they did not deny fascist [political repression](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Political_repression) and antisemitism and even justified them.[^cite_note-411]



Far right Russian demonstration

With the outbreak of World War II, Russian fascists in Germany supported Nazi Germany and joined the ranks of Russian collaborators.

Some Russian neo-Nazi organizations are part of the international [World Union of National Socialists](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/World_Union_of_National_Socialists) (WUNS, founded in 1962). As of 2012, six Russian organizations are among the officially registered members of the union: National Resistance, National Socialist Movement – Russian Division, All-Russian Public Patriotic Movement "[Russian National Unity](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Russian_National_Unity)", National Socialist Movement "[Slavic Union](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Slavic_Union_\(Russia\))" (prohibited by a court decision in June 2010), and others. The following organizations are not included in WUNS: the [National Socialist Society](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/National_Socialist_Society) (banned by a court decision in 2010), the [Russian All-National Union](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Russian_All-National_Union) (banned in September 2011), and others, such as skinheads: Legion Werewolf (liquidated in 1996), Schultz-88 (liquidated in 2006), White Wolves (liquidated in 2008–2010), New Order (ceased to exist), Russian goal (ceased to exist), and others. Some of the more radical neo-Nazi organizations, using terrorist methods, belonged to skinhead groups such as the Werewolf Legion (liquidated in 1996), Schultz-88 (liquidated in 2006), White Wolves (liquidated in 2008–2010), New Order (ceased to exist), "Russian Goal" (ceased to exist), and others.[^cite_note-412]

Until the end of the 1990s, one of the largest parties of Russian national extremists was the neo-Nazi socio-political movement "Russian National Unity" (RNE), founded by [Alexander Barkashov](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Alexander_Barkashov) in 1990. At the end of 1999, the RNE made an unsuccessful attempt to take part in the elections to the State Duma. Barkashov considered "true Orthodoxy" as a fusion of Christianity with paganism and advocated the "Russian God" and the "Aryan swastika" allegedly associated with it. He wrote about the Atlanteans, the Etruscans, and the "[Aryan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Aryan_race)" civilization as the direct predecessors of the Russian nation, in a centuries-old struggle with the "Semites", the "[world Jewish conspiracy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Judeo-Masonic_conspiracy_theory)", and the "dominance of the Jews in Russia". The symbol of the movement was a modified swastika. Barkashov was a parishioner of the "[True Orthodox ("Catacomb") Church](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Russian_True_Orthodox_Church_\(Lazar_Zhurbenko\))", and the first cells of the RNE were formed as brotherhoods and communities of the RTOC.[^cite_note-Schnirelmann2012-413]

The ideology of Russian neo-Nazism is closely connected with the ideology of [Slavic neo-paganism (rodnovery)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Slavic_Native_Faith). In a number of cases, there are also organizational ties between neo-Nazis and neo-pagans. One of the founders of Russian neo-paganism, the former dissident [Alexey Dobrovolsky](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Alexey_Dobrovolsky) (pagan name – Dobroslav) shared the ideas of Nazism and transferred them to his neo-pagan teaching.[^cite_note-Schnirelmann2012-413][^cite_note-Schnirelmann2015-414] Modern Russian neo-paganism took shape in the second half[^cite_note-415] of the 1970s and is associated with the activities of Dobrovolsky and Moscow Arabist [Valery Yemelyanov](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Valery_Yemelyanov) (neo-pagan name – Velemir),[^cite_note-416][^cite_note-Schnirelmann2015-414] both supporters of antisemitism. Rodnoverie is a popular religion among Russian [skinheads](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/White_power_skinhead).[^cite_note-\:0-417][^cite_note-418] These skinheads, however, do not usually practice their religion.[^cite_note-\:0-417]

Historian Dmitry Shlapentokh wrote that, as in Europe, neo-paganism in Russia pushes some of its adherents to antisemitism. This antisemitism is closely related to negative attitudes towards Asians, and this emphasis on racial factors can lead neo-pagans to neo-Nazism. The tendency of neo-pagans to antisemitism is a logical development of the ideas of neo-paganism and imitation of the Nazis, and is also a consequence of a number of specific conditions of modern Russian politics. Unlike previous regimes, the modern Russian political regime, as well as the ideology of the middle class, combines support for Orthodoxy with [philosemitism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Philosemitism) and a positive attitude towards Muslims. These features of the regime contributed to the formation of specific views of neo-Nazi neo-pagans, which are represented to a large extent among the socially unprotected and marginalized [Russian youth](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Russian_youth?action=edit\&redlink=1). In their opinion, power in Russia was usurped by a cabal of conspirators, including hierarchs of the Orthodox Church, Jews, and Muslims. Contrary to external differences, it is believed that these forces have united in their desire to maintain power over the Russian "Aryans".[^cite_note-419]

#### Serbia

Main article: [Far-right politics in Serbia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Far-right_politics_in_Serbia)



[Chetniks](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Chetniks) in Belgrade, 1920

In the [Kingdom of Yugoslavia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kingdom_of_Yugoslavia), multiple far-right organizations and parties operated during the late [Interwar period](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Interwar_period) such as the [Yugoslav National Movement](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Yugoslav_National_Movement) (Zbor), [Yugoslav Radical Union](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Yugoslav_Radical_Union) (JRZ) and [Organization of Yugoslav Nationalists](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Organization_of_Yugoslav_Nationalists) (ORJUNA). Zbor was headed by [Dimitrije Ljotić](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Dimitrije_Ljoti%C4%87), who during the [World War II](32927.md) collaborated with the [Axis powers](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Axis_powers).[^cite_note-420] Ljotić was a supporter of [Italian fascism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Italian_fascism),[^cite_note-Ramet2006-421] and he advocated for the establishment of a [centralized Yugoslav state](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Centralized_government) that would be dominated by Serbs, and a return to Christian traditions.[^cite_note-422] Zbor was the only registered political party in Yugoslavia that openly promoted [antisemitism](1078.md) and [xenophobia](34141.md).[^cite_note-423] JRZ was registered as a political party in 1934 by [Milan Stojadinović](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Milan_Stojadinovi%C4%87), a right-wing politician who expressed his support towards Italian fascism during his premiership.[^cite_note-424] JRZ was initially a coalition made up of Stojadinović's, [Anton Korošec](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Anton_Koro%C5%A1ec)'s and [Mehmed Spaho](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mehmed_Spaho)'s supporters, and the party was the main stronghold for [Yugoslav ethnic nationalists](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Yugoslavism) and supporters of [Karađorđević dynasty](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kara%C4%91or%C4%91evi%C4%87_dynasty).[^cite_note-425] ORJUNA was a prominent organization in the 1920s that was influenced by fascism.[^cite_note-Ramet2006-421] During World War II, [Chetniks](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Chetniks), an [ethnic ultranationalist](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ethnic_nationalism) movement rose to prominence.[^cite_note-426] Chetniks were staunchly anti-communist and they supported [monarchism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Monarchism) and the creation of a Greater Serbian state.[^cite_note-427][^cite_note-428] They, including their leader [Draža Mihailović](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Dra%C5%BEa_Mihailovi%C4%87), extensively collaborated with the Axis powers in the second half of the World War II against their common enemy, the [Yugoslav Partisans](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Yugoslav_Partisans).[^cite_note-429]

After the re-establishment of the multi-party system in [Serbia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Socialist_Republic_of_Serbia) in 1990, multiple right-wing movements and parties began getting popularity from which the [Serbian Radical Party](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Serbian_Radical_Party) was the most successful.[^cite_note-Ramet2006-421] [Vojislav Šešelj](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Vojislav_%C5%A0e%C5%A1elj), who founded the party, promoted popular notions of "international conspiracy against the Serbs" during the 1990s which gained him popularity in the [1992](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1992_Serbian_general_election) and [1997 election](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1997_Serbian_general_election).[^cite_note-430] During the 1990s, SRS has been also described as [neofascist](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Neofascism) due to their vocal support of [ethnic ultranationalism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ultranationalism) and [irredentism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Serbian_irredentism).[^cite_note-431][^cite_note-432] Its popularity went into decline after the [2008 election](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2008_Serbian_parliamentary_election) when its acting leader [Tomislav Nikolić](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tomislav_Nikoli%C4%87) seceded from the party to form the [Serbian Progressive Party](19454384.md).[^cite_note-433] Besides SRS, during the 2000s multiple neofascist and [Neo-Nazi](54361.md) movements began getting popular, such as [Nacionalni stroj](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nacionalni_stroj), [Obraz](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Obraz_\(organization\)) and [1389 Movement](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1389_Movement).[^cite_note-434] [Dveri](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Dveri), an organization turned political party, was also a prominent promoter of far-right content, and they were mainly known for their [clerical-fascist](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Clerical_fascism), [socially conservative](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Social_conservatism) and [anti-Western](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Anti-Western_sentiment) stances.[^cite_note-435][^cite_note-436] Since 2019, the far-right [Serbian Party Oathkeepers](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Serbian_Party_Oathkeepers) has gained popularity mainly due to their ultranationalist views,[^cite_note-437] including the openly neofascist [Leviathan Movement](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Leviathan_Movement).[^cite_note-438][^cite_note-439]

#### Slovenia

This section is an excerpt from [Far-right politics in Slovenia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Far-right_politics_in_Slovenia).\[[edit](https://en.wikipedia.org/w/index.php?title=Far-right_politics_in_Slovenia\&action=edit)]

There are multiple groups and organisations within [Slovenia](27338.md) which are or have been engaged in far-right political activity, and [right-wing extremism](428002.md). Their political activity has traditionally opposed and targeted socially progressive policies, and minorities (in particular; the LGBT community, and ethnic minorities like the [Roma](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Romani_people) and immigrants (particularly those from the Southern Balkans),[^cite_note-Far-right_politics_in_Slovenia_Kosak_Jul_12-440][^cite_note-Far-right_politics_in_Slovenia_\:13-441][^cite_note-Far-right_politics_in_Slovenia_borut_jul_09-442][^cite_note-443] and espoused traditional ultraconservative and [reactionary](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Reactionary) views and values.[^cite_note-Far-right_politics_in_Slovenia_Kosak_Jul_12-440][^cite_note-Far-right_politics_in_Slovenia_borut_jul_09-442] More recently, a rise in new, incipient alt-right groups has been noted, particularly as a reaction to the [European migrant crisis](46415102.md).[^cite_note-Far-right_politics_in_Slovenia_delo_sep_14-444]

#### Spain

This section is an excerpt from [History of the far-right in Spain](62493456.md).\[[edit](https://en.wikipedia.org/w/index.php?title=History_of_the_far-right_in_Spain\&action=edit)]

The [history of the far-right in Spain](62493456.md) dates back to at least the 1800s and refers to any manifestation of far-right politics in [Spain](26667.md). Individuals and organizations associated with the far-right in Spain often employ [reactionary](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Reactionary) [traditionalism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Traditionalism_\(Spain\)), religious [fundamentalism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fundamentalism), corporate [Catholicism](606848.md), and [fascism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fascism) in their ideological practice. In the case of Spain, according to historian Pedro Carlos González Cuevas, the predominance of Catholicism played an essential role in the suppression of external political innovations such as [Social Darwinism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Social_Darwinism), [positivism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Positivism), and [vitalism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Vitalism) in Spanish far-right politics.[^cite_note-445]

#### Switzerland

The [Swiss People's Party](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Swiss_People's_Party), one of the leading right-wing parties in Switzerland, is widely described as far-right.[^cite_note-446][^cite_note-447][^cite_note-448][^cite_note-449]

#### United Kingdom

Main article: [Far-right politics in the United Kingdom](3884621.md)

The British far-right rose out of the [fascist](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fascist) movement. In 1932, [Oswald Mosley](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Oswald_Mosley) founded the [British Union of Fascists](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/British_Union_of_Fascists) (BUF) which was banned during [World War II](32927.md).[^cite_note-450] Founded in 1954 by [A. K. Chesterton](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/A._K._Chesterton), the [League of Empire Loyalists](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/League_of_Empire_Loyalists) became the main British far-right group at the time. It was a [pressure group](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pressure_group) rather than a political party, and did not contest elections. Most of its members were part of the [Conservative Party](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Conservative_Party_\(UK\)) and were known for politically embarrassing stunts at party conferences.[^cite_note-451] Other fascist parties included the [White Defence League](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/White_Defence_League) and the [National Labour Party](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/National_Labour_Party_\(UK_1950s\)) who merged in 1960 to form the second [British National Party](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/British_National_Party_\(1960s\)) (BNP).[^cite_note-452]

With the decline of the [British Empire](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/British_Empire) becoming inevitable, British far-right parties turned their attention to internal matters. The 1950s had seen an increase in immigration to the UK from its former colonies, particularly India, Pakistan, the Caribbean and Uganda. Led by [John Bean](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/John_Bean_\(politician\)) and [Andrew Fountaine](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Andrew_Fountaine), the BNP opposed the admittance of these people to the UK. A number of its rallies such as one in 1962 in [Trafalgar Square](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Trafalgar_Square) ended in [race riots](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Race_riot). After a few early successes, the party got into difficulties and was destroyed by internal arguments.
